2.2.
Sound Bayesian Inference via Guide Types

Programs as coroutines

Our first contribution is a
coroutine
-based paradigm for implementing
the model and guide programs for Bayesian inference.
In an inference algorithm, the model program and its guide program have many connections.
The two most significant patterns we can observe in common inference algorithms are as follows:

•

The guide program is used to generate sample traces, and then the model program
is simulated with these traces to compute likelihoods.

•

The guide program needs to have similar control-flow structure to that of the model program.
For example, if the model program has a conditional command whose two branches sample different
sets of latent variables, the guide program should also have a conditional command with an
equivalent branch condition.

The first pattern illustrates a form of
sample passing
from the guide program to
the model program, and the second pattern indicates that the model program should provide

branch selection
to the guide program.
Such
bidirectional guidance
inspired us to treat the model and guide programs as

coroutines
that communicate with each other during their execution, rather than as
totally independent programs.
On the other hand, we do
not
want the coroutines to be tightly coupled: Bayesian practitioners
usually maintain a separation between the model and the guide so that they can refine the guide
iteratively to improve inference performance.

Therefore, we use
message-passing communication
to implement the coroutines;
this formalism allows us to separate the model and the guide as individual programs,
but connect them via
channels
over which coroutines exchange messages.

fig.

5
reimplements the model and guide programs in
fig.

1
and

fig.

3
, respectively, by making the guidance communication explicit.
The
\textnormal{{sample}}(\cdot)
commands and conditional commands are annotated with
\mathsf{rv}
(i.e., “receive”)
or
\mathsf{sd}
(i.e., “send”) to indicate the direction of communication, and associated with
a name of the channel on which the communication is carried out.
In this example, we use two channels:

latent
for communication between the guide and the model,
and
obs
for identifying observations in the model.
Every channel has a unique provider and a unique consumer.
Note that in this way we do
not
need to use labeled samples—as Pyro and some other PPLs
do—because the sampling sites are synchronized through guidance communication.

Operationally, when a coroutine is executing a command associated with a channel
c
, it resumes
the other coroutine that accesses channel
c
, until the other coroutine encounters a command
that also communicates on channel
c
.
Then they perform
synchronization
; for example,

•

When
Model
executes
\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}(\textnormal{{Gamma}}(2;1))
,
it resumes the other end of the
latent
channel, i.e., the coroutine
\textnormal{{Guide}}_{1}
,
until
\textnormal{{Guide}}_{1}
reaches the command
\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}(\textnormal{{Gamma}}(1;1))
.
Recall that the guide program is used in importance sampling;
thus, the coroutine
\textnormal{{Guide}}_{1}
draws a sample from the distribution
\textnormal{{Gamma}}(1;1)
,
and then sends it to the coroutine
Model
, which uses the sample and the prior
distribution
\textnormal{{Gamma}}(2;1)
to calculate the importance weight.

•

When
\textnormal{{Guide}}_{1}
executes the conditional command on line 3 (where the
\star
symbol
indicates that the branch selection is received from the other coroutine),
it resumes the other end of the
latent
channel, i.e., the coroutine
Model
,
until
Model
reaches the conditional command on line 3.
The coroutine
Model
is the sender of the branch selection;
thus, it evaluates the branch predicate
v<2
, and sends the result back to
\textnormal{{Guide}}_{1}
.

When the synchronization is completed, either coroutine can continue to execute.

Model

1

proc

Model
()
consume

latent

provide

obs
=

2

v

\leftarrow

\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}(\textnormal{{Gamma}}(2;1))
;

3

\textnormal{{if}}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}

v<2

then

4

_
\leftarrow

\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{obs}}\}(\textnormal{{Normal}}(-1;1))
;

5

\textnormal{{return}}(v)

6

else

7

m

\leftarrow

\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}(\textnormal{{Beta}}(3;1))
;

8

_
\leftarrow

\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{obs}}\}(\textnormal{{Normal}}(m;1))
;

9

\textnormal{{return}}(v)

Guide

1

proc

\textnormal{{Guide}}_{1}
()
consume
.
provide

latent
=

2

v

\leftarrow

\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}(\textnormal{{Gamma}}(1;1))
;

3

\textnormal{{if}}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}

\star

then

4

\textnormal{{return}}()

5

else

6

\_

\leftarrow

\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}(\textnormal{{Unif}})
;

7

\textnormal{{return}}()

Figure 5
.
Probabilistic programs as coroutines.

Guide types

Our second contribution is
guide types
that enforce guidance protocols between coroutines,
and an efficient algorithm that infers guide types from code.

We take inspiration from type systems for communication protocols in concurrent systems, such
as
session types

(
Honda 1993
;
Honda
et al. 1998
)
.
The key idea is to
structure
the sequence of guidance messages on a channel, rather than
describe it as a collection of unrelated messages.

We sketch some type constructors in our development of guide types.
The type
\boldsymbol{1}
types an ended channel, where no messages can be exchanged.
The type
A\mathbin{\binampersand}B
types a channel whose provider waits for a branch selection,
and continues with a protocol of type
A
or a protocol of type
B
based on the received selection.
The type
\tau\wedge A
types a channel whose provider samples and sends a random value
of type
\tau
, and then continues with a type
A
protocol.
The guide type for a channel is the same for the provider and the consumer of the channel,
but the two ends of a channel interpret the guide type for the channel
dually

(e.g., sends as receives).

With these three type constructors, we can express the protocols for the
latent
and
obs

channels shown in
fig.

5
as

(3)

latent

\displaystyle\!:\mathbb{R}_{+}\wedge(\boldsymbol{1}\mathbin{\binampersand}(\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}\wedge\boldsymbol{1})),

(4)

obs

\displaystyle\!:\mathbb{R}\wedge\boldsymbol{1}.

The provider and the consumer of the channel
latent
are the coroutines
\textnormal{{Guide}}_{1}
and

Model
, respectively.
From the provider
\textnormal{{Guide}}_{1}
’s perspective, the protocol shown as type (
3
)
guides
\textnormal{{Guide}}_{1}
to draw a
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-valued sample and send it on
latent
, then wait for
a branch selection, and finally end the communication on
latent
if the received branch
selection is then-branch, otherwise draw an
\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}
-valued sample before ending the communication.
The coroutine
\textnormal{{Guide}}_{1}
implements this guidance protocol exactly.
Meanwhile, from the consumer
Model
’s perspective, the type constructors have

dual
semantics, i.e.,

send
becomes
receive
and vice versa;
thus, the protocol for
latent
guides
Model
to receive an
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-valued sample, and then send out a
branch selection on channel
latent
; if
Model
selects the else-branch,
then it further receives an
\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}
-valued sample on channel
latent
.

The channel
obs
, whose provider is the coroutine
Model
, is used to identify observations in the
probabilistic model.
The coroutine
Model
accesses
obs
on lines 4 and 8, each of which lies in a branch of the conditional
command on line 3.
Because the conditional command is associated with
latent
, it should
not
bother with the
communication on channel
obs
;
thus, we require that the two branches of the conditional command have the same guidance protocol for

obs
.
The protocol shown as type (
4
) specifies that the coroutine
Model
produces a
single
\mathbb{R}
-valued observation, and
Model
implements this protocol exactly.

Recursion

Probabilistic programs can use recursion to express complex generative
models, such as a
probabilistic context-free grammar
(PCFG), which is a popular model for
constructing languages
(
Jelinek
et al. 1992
)
.

fig.

6
shows a recursive model that generates a random expression tree with two
constructors:
\mathsf{Const}(\cdot)
for leaf nodes and
\mathsf{Add}(\cdot;\cdot)
for internal nodes.

1

proc

Pcfg
()
consume

latent

provide
. =

2

k

\leftarrow

\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}(\textnormal{{Beta}}(3;1))
;

3

call

PcfgGen
(
k
)

4

5

proc

PcfgGen
(
k
)
consume

latent

provide
. =

6

u

\leftarrow

\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}(\textnormal{{Unif}})
;

7

\textnormal{{if}}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}

u<k

then

8

v

\leftarrow

\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}(\textnormal{{Normal}}(0;1))
;

9

\textnormal{{return}}(\mathsf{Const}(v))

10

else

11

lhs

\leftarrow

call

PcfgGen
(
k
);

12

rhs

\leftarrow

call

PcfgGen
(
k
);

13

\textnormal{{return}}(\mathsf{Add}(\textnormal{{lhs}};\textnormal{{rhs}}))

Figure 6
.
A recursive probabilistic model.

To support recursion in probabilistic programs, we add a standard recursive-type
constructor to guide types.
However,
composition
of the guide types from multiple procedure calls in a
non-tail-recursive program remains a challenge.
One straightforward approach is to add a sequencing type
A\fatsemi B
that
types a channel whose provider starts with a type
A
protocol and then continues
with a type
B
protocol, but such sequencing types will complicate the type
system, because they allow a guidance protocol to be described by
different

types.
For example, both
(\mathbb{R}\wedge\mathbb{R}\wedge\boldsymbol{1})
and

((\mathbb{R}\wedge\boldsymbol{1})\fatsemi(\mathbb{R}\wedge\boldsymbol{1}))

describe a channel whose provider sends two
\mathbb{R}
-valued random samples.

To sidestep the need for a nontrivial equivalence check in the type system, we adapt the idea of

type-level polymorphism
, and parameterize the guide type for a recursive
coroutine by a
continuation
type that describes the communication after
a procedure call to this coroutine returns.
For example, consider the following parametric type
\mathsf{R}[\cdot]
.

\mathsf{R}[X]\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}\wedge((\mathbb{R}\wedge X)\mathbin{\binampersand}\mathsf{R}[\mathsf{R}[X]]),

It specifies a guidance protocol by
prepending
messages to the
continuation protocol defined by the type parameter
X
.
The type
\mathsf{R}[X]
precisely describes the behavior of the
PcfgGen
coroutine shown in

fig.

6
:
the coroutine first receives an
\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}
-valued random sample (line 6);
evaluates and sends out a branch selection (line 7); and then based on the branch
selection, the coroutine either receives an
\mathbb{R}
-valued random sample (line 8)
and then returns (i.e., continues with the continuation protocol
X
), or
makes two recursive procedure calls (lines 11 and 12).
The guide type of the else-branch can be justified by backward reasoning:
at line 13, the coroutine returns (i.e., continues with the continuation protocol
X
);
at line 12, because the guide type
after
the procedure call is
X
,
we obtain the guide type
before
the procedure call by instantiating
\mathsf{R}
with
X
;
and at line 11, because the guide type
after
the procedure call is
\mathsf{R}[X]
,
we again instantiate
\mathsf{R}
, but with
\mathsf{R}[X]
, to derive the guide type of the else-branch.
Finally, for the coroutine
Pcfg
shown in
fig.

6
,
we derive
\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}\wedge\mathsf{R}[\boldsymbol{1}]
as the guidance protocol for channel
latent
.

Control-flow divergence.

In
fig.

5
, the model program
Model
and the guide program
\textnormal{{Guide}}_{1}

have very similar control flow.
In general, our type system permits the guide’s control-flow structure to diverge from the model’s,
as long as the two programs communicate with each other in a consistent way, i.e., the two
programs follow the same guidance protocol for the channel over which they communicate.
For example, the program below implements a part of a Bayesian linear-regression model with outliers
(
Cusumano-Towner et al. 2019
)
,
where the latent variable
prob_outlier
describes how likely a data point does not
conform to the linear relationship, and
is_outlier
is a Boolean-valued latent variable that
indicates if a data point is an outlier.

1

prob_outlier

\leftarrow

\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}(\textnormal{{Unif}})
;

2

is_outlier

\leftarrow

\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}(\textnormal{{Ber}}(\textnormal{{prob\_outlier}}))
;

3

\textnormal{{return}}()

For MCMC algorithms, the guide program generates a new random sample
from an old one; thus,
for better inference performance,
an MCMC guide usually behaves differently for
different old samples.
The following program implements a part of a guide that branches on
is_outlier

from the old sample
(
Cusumano-Towner et al. 2019
)
.
Intuitively, this guide proposes the negation (with a small amount of noise) of the old
is_outlier
,
which is bound to a program variable
old_is_outlier
;
i.e., if the old
is_outlier
is true (resp., false), then the guide is likely to propose false (resp., true).

1

prob_outlier

\leftarrow

\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}(\textnormal{{Beta}}(2;5))
;

2

if

old_is_outlier

then

3

is_outlier

\leftarrow

\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}(\textnormal{{Ber}}(0.1))
;

4

\textnormal{{return}}()

5

else

6

is_outlier

\leftarrow

\textnormal{{sample}}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{latent}}\}(\textnormal{{Ber}}(0.9))
;

7

\textnormal{{return}}()

Although the model and the guide have divergent control-flow structures,
in our type system, we can express the guidance protocol for channel
latent
as

\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}\wedge\mathbb{2}\wedge\boldsymbol{1}
; that is, both programs sample an

\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}
-valued random variable and then sample a Boolean-valued one.

Type inference

Guide types can be automatically inferred from code; in practice, they can still be used
as specifications of the programs for better understanding.
Our implementation can infer guide types for the examples mentioned so far, including
the recursive one shown in
fig.

6
.

3.
A Coroutine-Based PPL

In this section, we formulate a core monadic calculus for coroutine-based probabilistic programming.

Syntax

fig.

7
presents the grammar of basic types
\tau
, expressions
e
, values
v
,
commands
m
, and programs
\mathcal{D}
in the core calculus via abstract binding trees
(
Harper 2016
)
.
There is a modal distinction in the core language:
expressions describe
purely deterministic
computations, while
commands describe
probabilistic
computations.
Intuitively, we treat randomness as a kind of monadic effect
(
Moggi 1989
)
.

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\mathbb{1}\mid\mathbb{2}\mid\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}\mid\mathbb{R}_{+}\mid\mathbb{R}\mid\mathbb{N}_{n}\mid\mathbb{N}\mid\tau_{1}\to\tau_{2}\mid\mathsf{dist}(\tau)

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle\Coloneqq x\mid\mathsf{triv}\mid\mathsf{true}\mid\mathsf{false}\mid\mathsf{if}(e;e_{1};e_{2})\mid\bar{r}\mid\bar{n}\mid\mathsf{op}_{\Diamond}(e_{1};e_{2})

\displaystyle\mid\lambda(x.e)\mid\mathsf{app}(e_{1};e_{2})\mid\mathsf{let}(e_{1};x.e_{2})

\displaystyle\mid\textnormal{{Ber}}(e)\mid\textnormal{{Unif}}\mid\textnormal{{Beta}}(e_{1};e_{2})\mid\textnormal{{Gamma}}(e_{1};e_{2})

\displaystyle\mid\textnormal{{Normal}}(e_{1};e_{2})\mid\textnormal{{Cat}}(e_{1},\cdots,e_{n})\mid\textnormal{{Geo}}(e)\mid\textnormal{{Pois}}(e)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\mathsf{triv}\mid\mathsf{true}\mid\mathsf{false}\mid\bar{r}\mid\bar{n}\mid\mathsf{clo}(V,\lambda(x.e))

\displaystyle\mid\textnormal{{Ber}}(v)\mid\textnormal{{Unif}}\mid\textnormal{{Beta}}(v_{1};v_{2})\mid\textnormal{{Gamma}}(v_{1};v_{2})

\displaystyle\mid\textnormal{{Normal}}(v_{1};v_{2})\mid\textnormal{{Cat}}(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})\mid\textnormal{{Geo}}(v)\mid\textnormal{{Pois}}(v)

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\mathsf{ret}(e)\mid\mathsf{bnd}(m_{1};x.m_{2})\mid\mathsf{call}(f;e)

\displaystyle\mid\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(e)\mid\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(e)

\displaystyle\mid\mathsf{cond}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(m_{1};m_{2})\mid\mathsf{cond}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(e;m_{1};m_{2})

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\overrightarrow{\mathsf{fix}\{a;b\}(f.x.m)}

Figure 7
.
Syntax of the core calculus.

The purely deterministic fragment is a simply-typed lambda calculus augmented with

scalar
types (i.e., nullary products
\mathbb{1}
, Booleans
\mathbb{2}
, unit interval
\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}
,
positive real numbers
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, real numbers
\mathbb{R}
, integer rings
\mathbb{N}_{n}
,
and natural numbers
\mathbb{N}
),
as well as a
distribution
type
\mathsf{dist}(\tau)
.
The syntactic form
\mathsf{op}_{\Diamond}(e_{1};e_{2})
represents expressions that perform
built-in binary operations
\Diamond
on scalar values.
Inhabitants of
\mathsf{dist}(\tau)
are the primitive distributions from which probabilistic
programs can draw a random value of type
\tau
;
for example, Bernoulli distributions
\textnormal{{Ber}}(\cdot)
have type
\mathsf{dist}(\mathbb{2})
,
the uniform distribution on unit interval
Unif
has type
\mathsf{dist}(\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)})
,
and geometric distributions
\textnormal{{Geo}}(\cdot)
have type
\mathsf{dist}(\mathbb{N})
.
For each primitive distribution
d
, we assume that it admits two fields:

d.\mathrm{support}
and
d.\mathrm{density}
are the support and the density function
of the distribution, respectively.
In the core calculus, the type of a primitive distribution characterizes the support of the
distribution
precisely
: for a distribution
d
of type
\mathsf{dist}(\tau)
and a value
v
,
it holds that
v\in d.\mathrm{support}
if and only if
v
is an inhabitant of type
\tau
.
Primitive distributions can be generalized to density-carrying expressions
(
Bhat
et al. 2012
;
Bhat
et al. 2013
)

to further improve language expressibility.

The probabilistic fragment is a monadic calculus augmented with probabilistic constructs
and communication primitives for coroutine-based programming.
The
sampling
commands
\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(e)
and
\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(e)
first evaluate
the expression
e
to a primitive distribution
d
.
Then the
send
version
\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(d)
draws a value from
d
and sends it on
channel
a
, whereas the
receive
version
\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(d)
receives a value from
channel
a
and treats it as a sample from
d
.
The random samples can influence the
likelihoods
of computations; thus, randomness
can be seen as a source of side effects.
The
branching
commands also have a
send
version
\mathsf{cond}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(e;m_{1};m_{2})
,
which evaluates
e
to a Boolean value and sends it as the branch selection on channel
a
;
and a
receive
version
\mathsf{cond}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(m_{1};m_{2})
, which receives a branch selection from
channel
a
.
The syntactic form
\mathsf{call}(f;e)
represents a procedure call, where
f
is a procedure
name and
e
is the argument.

A probabilistic program
\mathcal{D}
is a collection of (mutually recursive) procedures,
each of which has the form
\mathsf{fix}\{a;b\}(f.x.m)
, where
f
is the procedure name,

x
is the parameter,
m
is a command that represents the procedure body,

a
is the name of the channel consumed by
f
, and
b
is the name of the channel provided by
f
.
Note that both
a
and
b
are optional; that is, the procedure
f
might not consume any channel,
and it might not provide any channel.

Semantics

We develop a big-step operational semantics for the core calculus.
Details of the semantics are included in

appendix

B
.
The evaluation judgments for expressions have the form
V\vdash e\mathrel{\Downarrow}v
, where
V
is an

environment
that maps program variables to values.
The evaluation rules for expressions are skipped here because they are standard.

We adopt a trace-based approach
(
Borgström et al. 2016
;
Kozen 1981
)
in our semantics of
probabilistic computations.
A
guidance trace

\sigma
is a finite sequence of guidance messages exchanged on a channel;
each
guidance message
has the form

\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{P}}(v)
(resp.,
\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{P}}(v)
) for a sample value
v
(resp., a branch selection
v
)
from the
p
rovider to the consumer, the form

\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{C}}(v)
(resp.,
\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{C}}(v)
) for a sample value
v
(resp., a branch selection
v
)
from the
c
onsumer to the provider,
or a procedure-call indicator
fold
.
1
1

1

The
fold
message is only useful in the theoretical development;
it can be seen as the introduction form for guidance traces whose type is a type-operator instantiation (see
section

4
).

The evaluation judgments for commands have the form
V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v
, where
V
is an environment,

m
is a command that consumes channel
a
and provides channel
b
,

\sigma_{a}
and
\sigma_{b}
are guidance traces on the channels,

v
is the evaluation result,
and
w\geq 0
is a
weight
that expresses how likely the guidance traces are.
Intuitively, a probabilistic program specifies a probability distribution on guidance traces,
and the weights represent
probability densities
with respect to the distribution.

\small\frac{V\vdash e\mathrel{\Downarrow}v}{V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[]);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[])\vdash\mathsf{ret}(e)\mathrel{\Downarrow^{1}}v}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{EM:Ret}{({EM:Ret})}},}\frac{V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m_{1}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{1}}}v_{1}\hskip 9.24994ptV[x\mapsto v_{1}]\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a}^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b}^{\prime})\vdash m_{2}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{2}}}v_{2}}{V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a}\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{a}^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b}\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{b}^{\prime})\vdash\mathsf{bnd}(m_{1};x.m_{2})\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{1}\cdot w_{2}}}v_{2}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{EM:Bnd}{({EM:Bnd})}},}\frac{V\vdash e\mathrel{\Downarrow}d\hskip 9.24994ptv\in d.\mathrm{support}\hskip 9.24994ptw=d.\mathrm{density}(v)}{V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{P}}(v)]);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[])\vdash\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(e)\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{EM:Sample:Recv:L}{({EM:Sample:Recv:L})}},}\frac{V\vdash e\mathrel{\Downarrow}d\hskip 9.24994ptv\in d.\mathrm{support}\hskip 9.24994ptw=d.\mathrm{density}(v)}{V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[]);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{P}}(v)])\vdash\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{b}}\}(e)\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{EM:Sample:Send:R}{({EM:Sample:Send:R})}},}\frac{V\vdash e\mathrel{\Downarrow}v_{e}\hskip 9.24994pti=\mathsf{ite}(v_{a},1,2)\hskip 9.24994ptV\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m_{i}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v}{V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{C}}(v_{a})]\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash\mathsf{cond}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(e;m_{1};m_{2})\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w\cdot[v_{a}=v_{e}]}}v}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{EM:Cond:Send:L}{({EM:Cond:Send:L})}},}\frac{i=\mathsf{ite}(v_{b},1,2)\hskip 9.24994ptV\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m_{i}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v}{V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{C}}(v_{b})]\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{b})\vdash\mathsf{cond}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{b}}\}(m_{1};m_{2})\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{EM:Cond:Recv:R}{({EM:Cond:Recv:R})}},}\frac{\mathcal{D}(f)=\mathsf{fix}\{a;b\}(f.x_{f}.m_{f})\hskip 9.24994ptV\vdash e\mathrel{\Downarrow}v_{1}\hskip 9.24994pt\emptyset[x_{f}\mapsto v_{1}]\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m_{f}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v_{2}}{V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[\textbf{{fold}}]\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[\textbf{{fold}}]\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{b})\vdash\mathsf{call}(f;e)\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v_{2}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{EM:Call}{({EM:Call})}},}

Figure 8
.
Selected evaluation rules for commands.

fig.

8
shows the evaluation rules for selected commands.
We use the following notational conventions.
We denote the empty environment by
\emptyset
, and
updating a binding of
x
in an environment
V
to
v
by
V[x\mapsto v]
.
We use the
\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}
operator to concatenate two traces.
We write
\mathsf{ite}
as a shorthand for
\mathsf{if{\text{-}}then{\text{-}}else}
.
The
Iverson brackets

[\cdot]
are defined by
[\varphi]=1
if
\varphi
is true
and otherwise
[\varphi]=0
.

The (
EM:Sample:*
) rules take a value from the guidance traces as the result of the sampling,
and use the density functions of primitive distributions to calculate the weight for the guidance traces.
The (
EM:Cond:Send:L
) rule evaluates the branch predicate to obtain a Boolean value,
and enforce that the branch selection from the guidance trace of the consumed channel must be
the same as the predicate’s value;
if the guidance trace sets the branch selection to a different value, we simply set the weight
of this trace to zero.
The (
EM:Call
) rule requires the guidance traces start with a
fold
message, and proceeds
by evaluating the body of the callee.

Example 3.1.

Consider the command

\displaystyle m_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}{}

\displaystyle\enskip\mathsf{bnd}(~\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(\textnormal{{Normal}}(0;1));~x.

\displaystyle\enskip\mathsf{bnd}(~\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{b}}\}(\textnormal{{Normal}}(x;1));~y.

\displaystyle\enskip\mathsf{ret}(\mathsf{op}_{+}(x;y))\enskip)\enskip),

which consumes a channel
a
and provides a channel
b
.
Let
\varphi\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\lambda x.\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}}
be the probability
density function of the standard normal distribution
\textnormal{{Normal}}(0;1)
.
Let
\sigma_{a}\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}[\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\bar{1})]
and
\sigma_{b}\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}[\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\bar{2})]
.
Then we can derive the evaluation judgment

\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m_{1}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{\varphi(1)\cdot\varphi(1)}}\bar{3},

for the command
m_{1}
and the guidance traces
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}
.

Communication

There are a lot of formalisms for communication in (concurrent) programming systems,
such as CCS
(
Milner 1989
)
, Theoretical CSP
(
Hoare 1978
)
,
and
\pi
-calculus
(
Milner
et al. 1992a
;
Milner
et al. 1992b
)
.
In this paper, we use a lightweight approach to handling communication;
that is, in the semantics, we assume we have all the messages exchanged on all the
communication channels.
We use this formalism because
(i) our focus is to reason about soundness of Bayesian inference, rather than concurrency-related
properties (e.g., deadlock freedom); and
(ii) the inference algorithms we study in
section

5
involve only two
coroutines—one for the model and the other for the guide—so the communication
in our system is much simpler than that in general concurrent systems.

Example 3.2.

Consider the command

m_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}{}\enskip\mathsf{bnd}(\enskip\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(\textnormal{{Normal}}(3;1));\enskip\_.\enskip\mathsf{ret}(\mathsf{triv})\enskip),

which provides a channel
a
that is consumed by the command
m_{1}
in
example

3.1
.
To model the communication between
m_{2}
and
m_{1}
, we simply use the guidance trace

\sigma_{a}=[\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\bar{1})]
as the sequence of messages exchanged on
channel
a
in the semantics, and derive evaluation judgments for
m_{2}
and
m_{1}

separately.
We showed the judgment for
m_{1}
in
example

3.1
; here, we
can derive the judgment

\emptyset\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a})\vdash m^{\prime}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{\varphi(-2)}}\mathsf{triv},

for command
m_{2}
and guidance trace
\sigma_{a}
.
We use the
\varnothing
symbol to indicate that
m_{2}
does not consume
any channel.

4.
Guide Types

Type formation

We take inspiration from a
structuring
principle in session types
(
Honda 1993
;
Honda
et al. 1998
)
,
and develop
guide types
to enforce protocols for guidance traces.
The grammar shown below formulates the syntax of guide types.
We write
A,B
for guide types,
X
for type variables,
T
for unary type operators, and

F
for procedure signatures.

\displaystyle A,B

\displaystyle\Coloneqq X\mid\boldsymbol{1}\mid T[A]\mid\tau\wedge A\mid\tau\supset A\mid A\mathbin{\varoplus}B\mid A\mathbin{\binampersand}B

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\tau_{1}\leadsto\tau_{2}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:T_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:T_{b})

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\overrightarrow{\mathsf{typedef}(T.X.A)}

The type
\boldsymbol{1}
indicates an ended channel, where the guidance trace is empty.
The type
T[A]
instantiates a unary type operator
T
with a guide type
A
.
For sample passing and branch selection, each type constructor has a dual version that reverses
the role of the provider and the consumer.
The type
\tau\wedge A
types a channel whose
provider
samples a random value
, sends it on the channel, and then continues with a type
A
guidance protocol;
dually, the type
\tau\supset A
types a channel whose
consumer
samples and sends
a random value.
Similarly, the type
A\mathbin{\varoplus}B
types a channel whose
provider
evaluates a branch
predicate, sends a branch selection on the channel, and then continues with a type
A
guidance
protocol or a type
B
protocol based on the branch selection;
dually, the type
A\mathbin{\binampersand}B
types a channel whose
consumer
evaluates and sends a
branch selection.

Remark 4.1.

In the rest of this paper, we will
not
use the dual types
\tau\supset A
and
A\mathbin{\varoplus}B
.
We introduce these types here for theoretical completeness, and they may be used in some future development.

Type operators prescribe guidance protocols for procedures by parameterizing with a continuation
type that describes the guidance protocol after a procedure call.
A procedure signature
\tau_{1}\leadsto\tau_{2}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:T_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:T_{b})
types a
procedure that takes a parameter of type
\tau_{1}
, returns a result of type
\tau_{2}
,
consumes a channel
a
, and provides a channel
b
,
such that
if the guidance protocols for
a
and
b

after
a procedure call are
A
and
B
, respectively,
then the guidance protocols for
a
and
b

before
the procedure call are
T_{a}[A]
and
T_{b}[B]
,
respectively.

A
type definition

\mathsf{typedef}(T.X.A)
declares a unary type operator
T
that takes a type
parameter
X
and produces a guide type
A
, which can reference
X
.
Because type operators are used to prescribe procedure signatures, we assume that a probabilistic
program is always accompanied by a collection
\mathcal{T}
of (mutually recursive) type definitions.

Example 4.2.

We can formally declare the type operator
\mathsf{Recur}
for the
PcfgGen
procedure shown
in
fig.

6
as

\mathsf{typedef}(~\mathsf{R}.~X.~\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}\wedge((\mathbb{R}\wedge X)\mathbin{\binampersand}\mathsf{R}[\mathsf{R}[X]])~).

Typing rules

The typing judgments for expressions have the form
\Gamma\vdash e:\tau
,
where
\Gamma
is a
typing context
that maps program variables to basic types (defined in
fig.

7
).
A full list of typing rules is included in

appendix

B
.
The typing rules for expressions are skipped here because they are standard.

The typing judgments for commands have the form

\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash_{\Sigma}m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime}),

where
\Sigma
maps procedure identifiers to procedure signatures.
The intuitive meaning of the typing judgment is that
if the channels
a
and
b
are of the guidance protocols
A
and
B
, respectively,
then we can evaluate the command
m
to a value of type
\tau
,
and after the evaluation, the channels
a
and
b
are of the guidance protocols
A^{\prime}

and
B^{\prime}
, respectively.

\small\frac{\Gamma\vdash e:\tau}{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash\mathsf{ret}(e)\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{TM:Ret}{({TM:Ret})}},}\frac{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m_{1}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,x:\tau_{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})\vdash m_{2}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{2}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime\prime})}{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash\mathsf{bnd}(m_{1};x.m_{2})\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{2}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime\prime})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{TM:Bnd}{({TM:Bnd})}},}\frac{\Gamma\vdash e:\mathsf{dist}(\tau)}{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\tau\wedge A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(e)\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{TM:Sample:Recv:L}{({TM:Sample:Recv:L})}},}\frac{\Gamma\vdash e:\mathsf{dist}(\tau)}{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\tau\wedge B)\vdash\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{b}}\}(e)\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{TM:Sample:Send:R}{({TM:Sample:Send:R})}},}\frac{\Gamma\vdash e:\mathbb{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A_{1});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m_{1}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A_{2});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m_{2}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})}{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A_{1}\mathbin{\binampersand}A_{2});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash\mathsf{cond}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(e;m_{1};m_{2})\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{TM:Cond:Send:L}{({TM:Cond:Send:L})}},}\frac{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B_{1})\vdash m_{1}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B_{2})\vdash m_{2}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})}{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B_{1}\mathbin{\binampersand}B_{2})\vdash\mathsf{cond}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{b}}\}(m_{1};m_{2})\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{TM:Cond:Recv:R}{({TM:Cond:Recv:R})}},}\frac{\Sigma(f)=\tau_{1}\leadsto\tau_{2}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:T_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:T_{b})\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash e:\tau_{1}}{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:T_{a}[A]);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:T_{b}[B])\vdash\mathsf{call}(f;e)\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{2}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{TM:Call}{({TM:Call})}},}

Figure 9
.
Selected typing rules for commands.

fig.

9
presents the typing rules for commands.
We assume a fixed global
\Sigma
that we omit from the rules.
Intuitively, the rules formulate a
backward
-reasoning system:
we start with continuation types
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
for the channels
a
and
b
, respectively,
and then prepend the guidance messages sent or received by the command
m
to
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
,
to obtain the guide types
A
and
B
for the channels
a
and
b
before the evaluation of
m
,
respectively.
For sample passing and branch selection, each guide type has two derivation rules:
one for the consumed channel
a
, and the other for the provided channel
b
.
For example, the type
\tau\wedge A
represents a channel whose provider sends a
sample of type
\tau
; thus, if the consumed channel
a
has such a type,
the rule (
TM:Sample:Recv:L
)
receives
a sample from the provider of
a
,
and if the provided channel
b
has such a type, the rule (
TM:Sample:Send:R
)

sends
a sample to the consumer of
b
.

The rule (
TM:Call
) handles procedure calls.
For a procedure call
\mathsf{call}(f;e)
, the rule fetches from
\Sigma
the procedure
f
’s signature

\tau_{1}\leadsto\tau_{2}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:T_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:T_{b})
,
and then instantiates the type operators
T_{a},T_{b}
with continuation types
A,B
, respectively,
to obtain the guide types
T_{a}[A]
and
T_{b}[B]
for the channels
a
and
b
before the procedure
call, respectively.

Example 4.3.

Consider the command

\displaystyle m_{3}\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}{}

\displaystyle\enskip\mathsf{bnd}(\enskip\mathsf{call}(f;k);\enskip\_.

\displaystyle\enskip\mathsf{bnd}(\enskip\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{$\textnormal{{Normal}}(0;1)$}}\}(a);\enskip\_.

\displaystyle\enskip\mathsf{bnd}(\enskip\mathsf{call}(f;k);\enskip\_.

\displaystyle\enskip\mathsf{ret}(\mathsf{triv})\enskip)\enskip)\enskip),

where the variable
k
has type
\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}
and the procedure
f
has signature

\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}\leadsto\mathbb{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:T);\varnothing,

i.e., the procedure
f
consumes channel
a
but does not provide any channel,
and channel
a
is associated with a type operator
T
.
Now we show that we can derive a typing judgment for
m_{3}

by backward reasoning.
First, by (
TM:Ret
), we have

k:\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});\varnothing\vdash_{\Sigma}\mathsf{ret}(\mathsf{triv})\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\mathbb{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});\varnothing.

Then by (
TM:Call
), we derive

k:\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:T[\boldsymbol{1}]);\varnothing\vdash_{\Sigma}\mathsf{call}(f;k)\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\mathbb{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});\varnothing.

Define
m_{4}\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\mathsf{bnd}(\mathsf{call}(f;k);\_.\mathsf{ret}(\mathsf{triv}))
.
Thus, by (
TM:Bnd
),

k:\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:T[\boldsymbol{1}]);\varnothing\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{4}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\mathbb{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});\varnothing.

Define
m_{5}\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\mathsf{bnd}(\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{rv}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(\textnormal{{Normal}}(0;1));\_.m_{4})
.
By (
TM:Sample:Recv:L
) and (
TM:Bnd
), we have

k:\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\mathbb{R}\wedge T[\boldsymbol{1}]);\varnothing\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{5}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\mathbb{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});\varnothing.

Finally, we again apply (
TM:Call
) and (
TM:Bnd
) to derive

k:\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:T[\mathbb{R}\wedge T[\boldsymbol{1}]]);\varnothing\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{3}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\mathbb{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});\varnothing.

Type safety

We present some theoretical results about type safety of guide types.
Proofs are included in

appendix

B
.

We first formulate two judgments for well-formedness of values and guidance traces.
The judgment
v:\tau
means that value
v
has type
\tau
.
The judgment
\sigma:A
means that the guidance trace is a sequence of messages
that satisfies protocol
A
.
Rules for these judgments are straightforward; we omit them here but include them
in

appendix

B
.

The theorem below states that if
m
is a well-typed closed command,
and it evaluates to a value
v
under guidance traces
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}
,
then
v
is a well-typed value, and
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}
are well-typed guidance traces.

Theorem 4.4.

If
\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash_{\Sigma}m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\boldsymbol{1})

and
\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v
,
then

\sigma_{a}:A
,

\sigma_{b}:B
,
and
v:\tau
.

Furthermore, we can show some
normalization
properties of guide types.
The theorem below states that if
m
is a well-typed closed command,
and
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}
are well-typed guidance traces, then
m
can evaluate to
some well-typed
v
under
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}
.

Theorem 4.5.

If

\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash_{\Sigma}m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\boldsymbol{1})
,

\sigma_{a}:A
,
and
\sigma_{b}:B
,
then
there exist
w,v
such that

\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v
and

v:\tau
.

We can strengthen the normalization property when a command will
not
send out
any branch selections.
The theorem below states that
if a well-typed command
m
consumes a channel
a
with a type
A
that does not contain
\mathbin{\binampersand}
and provides a channel
b
with a type
B
that does not contain
\mathbin{\varoplus}
,
and
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}
are well-typed guidance traces,
then
m
can evaluate to some well-typed value
v
under
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}

with a
strictly positive
weight
w
.

Theorem 4.6.

If

\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash_{\Sigma}m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\boldsymbol{1})
,

A
is
\mathbin{\binampersand}
-free,

B
is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free,

\sigma_{a}:A
,
and
\sigma_{b}:B
,
then
there exist
w,v
such that

\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v
,

v:\tau
,
and
w>0
.

Type-inference algorithm

We now sketch a type-inference algorithm that derives guide types automatically
from the implementation.
In the algorithm, we assume we have information about basic types—such as the parameter
and result types for procedures and the typing contexts that map program variables to
basic types—because without guide types, our core language is a simply-typed lambda
calculus, for which type inference is decidable.

First, for each procedure
\mathsf{fix}\{a;b\}(f.x.m)
in the program,
we create two fresh type operators
T_{a}
and
T_{b}
for the channels
a
and
b
, respectively,
and obtains
\tau_{1}\leadsto\tau_{2}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:T_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:T_{b})
as the
signature of this procedure.
Then we collect signatures of all the procedures in the program to obtain the map
\Sigma
.

Now the task is to derive definitions of the type operators.
We observe that the rules in
fig.

9
are syntax directed, and
they can be turned into an algorithmic system by interpreting

\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash_{\Sigma}m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})

as a function from
\Sigma,\Gamma,m,\tau,a,b,A^{\prime},B^{\prime}
to
A,B
; i.e.,
we assume we know all the basic types, and we perform backward reasoning to infer
guide types.
Therefore, for each procedure
\mathsf{fix}\{a;b\}(f.x.m)
with
signature
\tau_{1}\leadsto\tau_{2}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:T_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:T_{b})
,
we create two fresh type variables
X_{a}
and
X_{b}
, derive two guide types

A
and
B
through

x:\tau_{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash_{\Sigma}m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{2}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:X_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:X_{b}),

and then add type definitions
\mathsf{typedef}(T_{a}.X_{a}.A)
and
\mathsf{typedef}(T_{b}.X_{b}.B)
.

5.
Soundness of Bayesian Inference

In this section, we use guide types to reason about Bayesian inference.
We first present a measure-theoretic formulation of Bayesian inference in the
coroutine-based PPL, and prove that guide types are
certificates

of absolute continuity (
section

5.1
).
We then sketch how guide types ensure key soundness conditions for multiple
Bayesian-inference algorithms (
section

5.2
).

Appendix

C

includes the details (e.g., formalizations and proofs) of this section.

5.1.
Verification of Absolute Continuity

We use the following notions from measure theory:

\sigma
-algebras, measurable spaces, measurable functions, measures,
and Lebesgue integration.

Appendix

A
provides a review of these notions.

Semantic domains

For each scalar type
\tau
, we equip it with a
standard Borel space

\llbracket\tau\rrbracket

on the inhabitants of
\tau
, i.e.,
\llbracket\tau\rrbracket
is a measurable space isomorphic
to a countable set or the real line.
We then equip each type
\tau
with a
stock measure

\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket}
:
if
\llbracket\tau\rrbracket
is a countable set, we define
\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket}
to be the counting measure;
otherwise,
\llbracket\tau\rrbracket
is a subset of the real line, so we define
\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket}
to be
the Lebesgue measure.

Because guidance traces are finite sequences of messages that
contain values of scalar types,
we can define
\llbracket A\rrbracket
as a standard Borel space on guidance traces that satisfy protocol
A
.
We then construct the stock measure
\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}
for
A
by decomposing
A
to products and/or sums of
scalar types, and then combining the stock measures for scalar types via product and/or coproduct measures.

Denotation of commands

For a well-typed closed command
m
, i.e.,

\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash_{\Sigma}m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\boldsymbol{1})
,
we define the
density function
of
m
as

\mathbf{P}_{m}(\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b})\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\begin{cases}w&if $\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v$\\
0&otherwise\end{cases}.

We can prove that
\mathbf{P}_{m}
is a measurable function from
\llbracket A\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket
—the
product measurable space of
\llbracket A\rrbracket
and
\llbracket B\rrbracket
—to nonnegative real numbers.
Thus, we construct a measure denotation
\llbracket m\rrbracket
for
m
, by integrating
\mathbf{P}_{m}
with
respect to the stock measure on the product space
\llbracket A\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket
, i.e.,

\llbracket m\rrbracket(S_{a,b})\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\int_{S_{a,b}}\mathbf{P}_{m}(\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket}(d(\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b})),

where
S_{a,b}
is a measurable set in
\llbracket A\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket
.

Bayesian inference

Let us fix a well-typed model program
m_{\mathsf{m}}
that consumes latent random variables on
a channel
latent
and provides observations on a channel
obs
, i.e.,

\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:B)\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{m}}\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}).

Usually, the program
m_{\mathsf{m}}
does
not
receive any branch selections, i.e.,

A
is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free and
B
is
\mathbin{\binampersand}
-free.
Given a concrete observation
\sigma_{o}:B
such that
\int\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})>0
,
Bayesian inference is the problem of approximating the
posterior

\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}
,
a measure
conditioned
with respect to
\sigma_{o}
, defined by

(5)

\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}(S_{\ell})\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\frac{\int_{S_{\ell}}\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})}{\int\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})},

where
S_{\ell}
is a measurable set in
\llbracket A\rrbracket
, i.e., a set of guidance traces of type
A
.
Note that if we fix the observation
\sigma_{o}
, then the denominator of
eq.

5

is a constant independent of
S_{\ell}
.
Thus, it is sufficient for an inference algorithm to ignore the denominator and approximate the measure

S_{\ell}\mapsto\int_{S_{\ell}}\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})
.

Guide programs

Bayesian-inference algorithms usually require some guide programs, such as
proposals for importance sampling and
approximating families for variational inference.
These guide programs specify measures on latent random
variables; in our system, we implement a guide program
m_{\mathsf{g}}
as
a coroutine that works with the model program
m_{\mathsf{m}}
and provides the

latent
channel with guide type
A
that
m_{\mathsf{m}}
consumes, i.e.,

\displaystyle\cdot\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A)\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{g}}\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}),

\displaystyle\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:B)\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{m}}\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}).

The guide and model have the
same
guide type
A
on channel
latent
.
Because the guide
provides
the channel and the model
consumes
the channel,
the two programs interpret the guide type
A

dually
; thus, their communication is compatible.

The coroutine-based paradigm folds the model and guide programs into a single entity;
thus, during the inference, both the model and guide coroutines execute.
To model
possible
combinations of traces for a model-guide system, we introduce
a reduction relation
V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\mathrel{\vdash_{\mathsf{red}}}m\mathrel{\Downarrow}v
,
where
V
is an environment,
m
is a command,
\sigma_{a}
and
\sigma_{b}
are guidance traces on channel
a
and channel
b
, respectively, and
v
is the reduction result.
The reduction relation is essentially the same as the evaluation relation for the operational semantics,
except that reduction does
not
account for probabilities.
Below are two example rules.

\small\frac{V\vdash e\mathrel{\Downarrow}d\hskip 9.24994ptv\in d.\mathrm{support}\hskip 9.24994pt}{V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[]);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{P}}(v)])\mathrel{\vdash_{\mathsf{red}}}\mathsf{sample}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{b}}\}(e)\mathrel{\Downarrow}v}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{RM:Sample:Send:R}{({RM:Sample:Send:R})}},}\frac{V\vdash e\mathrel{\Downarrow}v_{e}\hskip 18.49988pti=\mathsf{ite}(v_{e},1,2)\hskip 18.49988ptV\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\mathrel{\vdash_{\mathsf{red}}}m_{i}\mathrel{\Downarrow}v}{V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{C}}(v_{e})]\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\mathrel{\vdash_{\mathsf{red}}}\mathsf{cond}_{\mathsf{sd}}\{\textnormal{{a}}\}(e;m_{1};m_{2})\mathrel{\Downarrow}v}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{RM:Cond:Send:L}{({RM:Cond:Send:L})}},}

With the reduction relation, we say that a combination of traces
(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})
is

possible
for the model program
m_{\mathsf{m}}
and the guide program
m_{\mathsf{g}}
, if

\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\mathrel{\vdash_{\mathsf{red}}}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}v_{\mathsf{m}}
and
\emptyset\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell})\mathrel{\vdash_{\mathsf{red}}}m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}v_{\mathsf{g}}
for some values
v_{\mathsf{m}}
and
v_{\mathsf{g}}
.
We prove a lemma that connects the reduction relation with command denotations.

Lemma 5.1.

Suppose that
A
is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free,
B
is
\mathbin{\binampersand}
-free, and

\displaystyle\cdot\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{g}}\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}),

\displaystyle\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:B)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{m}}\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}).

Then a combination of traces
(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})
is possible for the model
m_{\mathsf{m}}
and the guide
m_{\mathsf{g}}

if and only if

\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\neq 0
.

We can now define a denotation for the guide
m_{\mathsf{g}}
,
accompanied by the model
m_{\mathsf{m}}
and conditioned
on a concrete observation
\sigma_{o}:B
, as a measure defined on possible traces:

\llbracket m_{\mathsf{g}}\rrbracket^{m_{\mathsf{m}}}_{\sigma_{o}}(S_{\ell})\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\int_{S_{\ell}}[\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\neq 0]\cdot\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{g}}}(\sigma_{\ell})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell}),

where
S_{\ell}
is a measurable set in
\llbracket A\rrbracket
.

Absolute continuity

A measure
\mu
is said to be
absolutely continuous
with respect to a measure
\nu
,
if
\mu
and
\nu
are defined on the same measurable space, and

\nu(S)\neq 0
for every measurable set
S
for which
\mu(S)\neq 0
.

We prove that for a model-guide pair, guide types serve as certificates for
absolute continuity.

Theorem 5.2.

Suppose that
A
is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free,
B
is
\mathbin{\binampersand}
-free,

\displaystyle\cdot\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{g}}\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}),

\displaystyle\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:B)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{m}}\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}),

and
\sigma_{o}:B
such that

\int\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})>0
.
Then
the measure
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
the measure
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{g}}\rrbracket^{m_{\mathsf{m}}}_{\sigma_{o}}
, and vice versa.

5.2.
Soundness of Inference Algorithms

We now describe how guide types can help us reason about
inference algorithms.

Importance sampling (IS)

IS approximates the posterior distribution by drawing latent variables using the guide program,
and then reweights the samples by their
importance
.
The operational rule below formulates a single step in the algorithm:
given a model program
m_{\mathsf{m}}
, a guide program
m_{\mathsf{g}}
, and a concrete observation
\sigma_{o}
,
IS performs joint execution of the two programs to draw a sample
\sigma_{\ell}
with density
w_{\mathsf{g}}

and compute
\frac{w_{\mathsf{m}}}{w_{\mathsf{g}}}
as the importance of
\sigma_{\ell}
.

\small\frac{\emptyset\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell})\vdash m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{\mathsf{g}}}}\_\hskip 9.24994pt\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\vdash m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{\mathsf{m}}}}\_}{m_{\mathsf{g}};m_{\mathsf{m}};\sigma_{o}\vdash_{\textsc{is}}^{w_{\mathsf{g}}}\langle\sigma_{\ell},\nicefrac{{w_{\mathsf{m}}}}{{w_{\mathsf{g}}}}\rangle}

By
theorem

5.2
, if the model and guide programs are well-typed, then
the posterior
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to

\llbracket m_{\mathsf{g}}\rrbracket^{m_{\mathsf{m}}}_{\sigma_{o}}
;
thus, IS is able to sample any possible latent variables
\sigma_{\ell}
in the posterior.
With the importance ratios, IS can be seen as generating
\sigma_{\ell}
with density

w_{\mathsf{g}}\cdot\frac{w_{\mathsf{m}}}{w_{\mathsf{g}}}=w_{\mathsf{m}}
. Thus, IS generates a measure proportional to
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}
.

Markov-Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC)

MCMC uses a transition kernel to generate iteratively a new random sample from an old one.
A popular MCMC algorithm is
Metropolis-Hastings
(MH), which constructs the transition
kernel from a
proposal
subroutine.
To implement proposal subroutines in our system, we extend the core calculus such that guidance
traces can be used as first-class data.
Then we implement the proposal subroutine as a procedure
g
whose argument is a guidance trace
on the channel for latent random variables.
The operational rule below formulates a single step in the MH algorithm;
given a proposal procedure
g
, a model
m_{\mathsf{m}}
, an observation
\sigma_{o}
,
and the current latent trace
\sigma_{\ell}
, MH first performs joint execution of
\mathsf{call}(g;\sigma_{\ell})

and
m_{\mathsf{m}}
to generate a new latent trace
\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime}
with density
w_{\mathsf{fwd}}
,
and then uses the new
\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime}
and the old
\sigma_{\ell}
to calculate a
backward
density
w_{\mathsf{bwd}}
.
MH then computes an
acceptance ratio

\alpha\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\min(1,\frac{w^{\prime}_{\mathsf{m}}\cdot w_{\mathsf{bwd}}}{w_{\mathsf{m}}\cdot w_{\mathsf{fwd}}})
, and accepts
the new sample
\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime}
with probability
\alpha
.

\small\frac{\emptyset\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime})\vdash\mathsf{call}(g;\sigma_{\ell})\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{\mathsf{fwd}}}}\_\hskip 9.24994pt\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\vdash m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w^{\prime}_{\mathsf{m}}}}\_\hskip 9.24994pt\emptyset\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell})\vdash\mathsf{call}(g;\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime})\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{\mathsf{bwd}}}}\_\hskip 9.24994pt\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\vdash m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{\mathsf{m}}}}\_}{g;m_{\mathsf{m}};\sigma_{o}\vdash_{\textsc{mh}}\sigma_{\ell}\xRightarrow{w_{\mathsf{fwd}}\cdot\alpha}\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime}}

Similar to IS, MH requires that the command
\mathsf{call}(g;\sigma_{\ell})
be able to sample any
possible latent variables
\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime}
in the posterior.
We prove the soundness of MH by a variant of
theorem

5.2
, where the programs do
not need to be closed so that they can reference data in the environment (e.g., the old samples).

Variational inference (VI)

VI uses optimization to find a candidate from an approximating family of guide programs that
minimizes the distance from the posterior distribution to the guide distribution.
We focus on verifying if the distance is well-defined, whereas VI requires extra conditions
for the optimization problem to be well-formed.
Here, we parameterize the guide
m_{\mathsf{g},\theta}
by a vector
\theta\in\Theta
of parameters,
and use KL divergence as the distance, which is defined by

\mathrm{KL}(\mu\parallel\nu)\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\int p_{\mu}(\sigma_{\ell})(\log p_{\mu}(\sigma_{\ell})-\log p_{\nu}(\sigma_{\ell}))\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell}),

where
\mu
and
\nu
are measures on
\llbracket A\rrbracket
with
densities
p_{\mu}
and
p_{\nu}
, respectively,
and
\mu
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\nu
.
The rule below formulates the computation of KL divergence for a specific
\theta
,
via joint execution of the two programs.

\small\frac{\emptyset\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell})\vdash m_{\mathsf{g},\theta}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{\mathsf{g}}}}\_\hskip 9.24994pt\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\vdash m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{\mathsf{m}}}}\_}{m_{\mathsf{g},\theta};m_{\mathsf{m}};\sigma_{o}\vdash_{\textsc{vi}}^{w_{\mathsf{g}}}\langle\sigma_{\ell},\log w_{\mathsf{m}}-\log w_{\mathsf{g}}\rangle}

The rule can be seen as defining a map
\sigma_{\ell}\mapsto w_{\mathsf{g}}\cdot(\log w_{\mathsf{m}}-\log w_{\mathsf{g}})
,
which is the integrand of the divergence
\mathrm{KL}(\llbracket m_{\mathsf{g},\theta}\rrbracket^{m_{\mathsf{m}}}_{\sigma_{o}}\parallel\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}})
.
By
theorem

5.2
, if the model and guide programs are well-typed,
then
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{g},\theta}\rrbracket^{m_{\mathsf{m}}}_{\sigma_{o}}
is absolutely continuous
with respect to
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}
; thus, the KL divergence used in VI
is well-defined.

6.
Experimental Evaluation

Implementation

We implemented the coroutine-based PPL in OCaml.
Our implementation consists of about 2,000 LOC; it contains a parser,
a type checker with automatic inference of guide types, and a prototype
compiler from our PPL to Pyro
(
Bingham
et al. 2018
)
.
Our implementation extends the core calculus with tensors (i.e.,
multi-dimensional matrices) and primitive iteration operators for them.
The prototype compiler supports code generation for importance sampling
and variational inference.
We use the Python package
greenlet

(
Website 2020
)
to support
coroutines in the compiled code.

Evaluation setup

We evaluated our implementation to answer the following two research
questions:

(1)

How expressive is the coroutine-based PPL, compared to a state-of-the-art
probabilistic programming language that ensures soundness of programmable inference
(
Lew et al. 2019
)
?

(2)

How efficient is our implementation, in terms of the time for type
inference, and the performance of Bayesian inference on the compiled code?

For the first question, we obtained 23 benchmarks from prior work
(
Lew et al. 2019
)

and collected 6 new benchmarks. The 29 benchmark programs consist of
(i) example models from Anglican
(
Wood
et al. 2014
)
, Turing
(
Ge
et al. 2018
)
,
and Pyro
(
Bingham
et al. 2018
)
, as well as
(ii) PCFG models, including a Gaussian-process domain-specific language
(DSL)
(
Saad et al. 2019
)
and synthetic models (such as examples shown in this paper).
Compared to prior work
(
Lew et al. 2019
)
, a larger subset of benchmark models
are expressible and type-checked in our PPL.
Particularly, our PPL is capable of expressing models with recursion and general conditional
branches, whereas prior work
(
Lew et al. 2019
)
is not.

For the second question, we ran Bayesian inference on the compiled code,
and compared the performance with non-coroutine-based, but equivalent, Pyro code.
We obtained guide programs from where we obtained the benchmark models,
and then reimplemented them in our PPL; for example, we implemented the encoder component
of a variational autoencoder as the guide program
(
Bingham
et al. 2018
)
.
For those benchmark models without guides, we first invoked our PPL to type-check the model
program and infer a guide type for the model, and then implemented a guide program whose type
was the guide type.
The compiled model and guide use Pyro’s primitives (such as
pyro.sample
) to sample
random data and condition on given data, as well as exchange messages and switch control with
each other using the concurrent-programming package
greenlet
.
We leveraged Pyro’s inference engines to carry out importance sampling or variational inference.
Type inference is very fast in practice; our implementation completed the
type-inference phase in several milliseconds on all of the benchmarks.
Our experiments showed that coroutines (implemented via messaging passing) do not introduce significant overhead
in actual Bayesian inference.

The experiments were performed on a machine with an Intel Core i7 3.6GHz processor
and 16GB of RAM under macOS Catalina 10.15.7.

Results

table

gives an overview of selected benchmark models.
Our benchmarks cover a wide range of Bayesian models, such as linear regression,
Gaussian mixtures, hidden Markov models, Bayesian networks, and variational
autoencoders.
Our benchmarks also include the classic Marsaglia algorithm (which generates a
normal distribution from a uniform distribution), a Poisson-trace algorithm
(shown in
fig.

10
, which generates a Poisson distribution from a uniform distribution), and a
Gaussian-process DSL (which uses a PCFG to generate the kernel function of a
Gaussian process).

As shown in
table

1
, our coroutine-based PPL is capable of
expressing most of the benchmarks, except those involving stochastic
memoization
(
Goodman et al. 2008
)
, such as the program
dp
.
The programs
branching
,
marsaglia
,
ptrace
, and
ex-1

have nontrivial branching, and the programs
marsaglia
,
ptrace
,

ex-2
, and
gp-dsl
define recursive models;
our implementation successfully inferred guide types for these programs,
whereas prior work
(
Lew et al. 2019
)
could not express them.
Our implementation derived guide types for 25 of the 29 benchmarks,
whereas prior work was able to express only 18 of them.

For all the benchmarks, we assume that each guide program
samples random variables in the
same
order as its corresponding model program does.
However, this assumption can sometimes be too restrictive: it has been shown that
the ability to allow the model and the guide to sample random variables in
different

orders is desirable for inference amortization methods
(
Webb et al. 2018
)
.
Prior work
(
Lew et al. 2019
)
allows different sampling orders in the model and the guide,
whereas our system cannot handle such scenarios.

table

presents performance statistics of selected benchmark programs.
We evaluated our PPL’s performance under two criteria:
(i) the time for type inference and code generation, and
(ii) the time for Bayesian inference compared to handwritten inference code under the
same set of hyperparameters (e.g., iteration rounds, optimization algorithms, and initial
values of parameters).
Our experiments showed that our implementation usually completes type inference and
code generation in several milliseconds, and the compiled code, although using coroutines,
has similar performance to handwritten inference code.

7.
Related Work

Sound Bayesian inference

Most closely related to our work are techniques for reasoning about soundness
of trace-based programmable inference.

Lee
et al. 2019
developed a static analysis of stochastic variational inference
with guide programs, which describe custom approximating families in Pyro.
Their analysis supports nontrivial features of Pyro, such as tensor manipulation and

plates
, i.e.,

vectors of conditionally independent samples.
Their approach aims at proving that the model and guide programs have
the same support and satisfy differentiability-related conditions.
Their static analysis does not handle the case when a conditional statement
determines the set of random samples.

Lew et al. 2019
proposed
trace types
as precise signatures for
sampling traces of probabilistic programs, and then used the type system
to prove absolute continuity in multiple kinds of inference algorithms.
Trace types can be seen as a type-and-effect system, where a trace type records
the precise set of samples drawn by a single program.
Trace types support higher-order functions,
stochastic
branches that can influence the set of random samples,
as well as three forms of loops, including stochastic while-loops with an unbounded number of iterations, but not general recursion.
Because the value
of a conditional predicate cannot be determined in general at static-analysis time, trace
types do not support general conditional statements that can influence the set of random samples.
Both
Lee
et al. 2019
’s and
Lew et al. 2019
’s approach allow the model and the guide to sample
random variables in different orders.

In this paper, we propose a new PPL that guarantees absolute
continuity between a model-guide pair, and features general programming
constructs, including recursion and branching.
A key innovation of our work is the coroutine-based paradigm of writing
inference code;
this paradigm makes the relational reasoning of the support-match property
explicit, and in particular enables precise analysis of complex control flow.
However, compared to prior work, our system only supports
scenarios where the model and the guide sample random variables in the same order.

There has been a line of work on validating Monte-Carlo inference algorithms.

Ścibior
et al. 2017
developed a semantic framework to verify the
soundness of Monte-Carlo inference algorithms with generic proposal distributions.

Atkinson
et al. 2018
presented a type system for verifying hand-coded Monte-Carlo
algorithms that explicitly manipulate densities, rather than use proposal distributions.
For MCMC methods,
Borgström et al. 2016
and
Hur
et al. 2015
developed provably correct MH algorithms.

Castellan and
Paquet 2019
proposed an
intensional
semantics, which captures
execution traces of programs, to validate an incremental MH algorithm.
Several systems
(
Atkinson
et al. 2018
;
Lew et al. 2019
;
Huang
et al. 2017
;
Bonawitz 2008
)
studied
sound combinators for kernels used by MCMC.
In contrast to the aforementioned work, our PPL is based on trace-based programmable inference.
It would be interesting to
develop programmable versions of those sound inference algorithms
in our PPL.

Narayanan et al. 2016
and
Zinkov and Shan 2017
validated the soundness of program transformations
in Hakaru, which contains a programmable MH algorithm.
The development of Hakaru is not centered around sample traces, and it
uses
symbolic disintegration

(
Shan and Ramsey 2017
;
Chang and Pollard 1997
)
to calculate
the marginal densities for computing the acceptance ratio in an MH step.
In this paper, we focus on a trace-based scheme for programmable inference.
Establishing the relationship among different schemes of programmable inference
is an interesting future research direction.

Session types

Honda et al.
(
Honda 1993
;
Honda
et al. 1998
)
introduced session types to
prescribe binary communication protocols for message-passing
processes.
Session types can be interpreted either classically
(
Wadler 2012
)
, or
intuitionistically
(
Caires and
Pfenning 2010
;
Caires
et al. 2016
)
.
To enable non-binary communication, researchers proposed
multiparty session types
(
Honda
et al. 2008
;
Coppo et al. 2015
;
Scalas and
Yoshida 2019
)
.
The tail-recursive structure of standard session types imposes communication
protocols that can be described by a
regular
language.
Recently, several systems have been developed to go beyond tail-recursive protocols,
such as context-free
(
Thiemann and
Vasconcelos 2016
)
, label-dependent
(
Thiemann and
Vasconcelos 2019
)
, and nested
(
Das
et al. 2020
)

session types.

In our development of guide types, we took inspiration from the structuring principle
of session types.
Compared to session types, guide types have different semantics (i.e., sending
and receiving random samples drawn from probability distributions),
have simpler forms (i.e., no process spawning or higher-order channels),
and enjoy an efficient type-inference algorithm, which can also analyze
non-tail-recursive communication protocols.
Developing a truly concurrent probabilistic programming system, and
concurrent Bayesian inference algorithms with general session types,
would be interesting future work.

8.
Conclusion

We have presented a new probabilistic programming language that supports
programmable Bayesian inference, and guarantees model-guide absolute continuity,
thereby ensuring key soundness properties of multiple kinds of inference algorithms.
Our language implements the model and guide programs as
coroutines
,
and we develop
guide types
to prescribe the communication protocols between
coroutines.
We have proved that well-typed model and guide coroutines execute safely, and they
are guaranteed to enjoy absolute continuity.
We have also developed an efficient type-inference algorithm that reconstructs
guide types directly from the code.
Finally, we have implemented our language with a prototype compiler to Pyro, and
evaluated our implementation on a suite of diverse probabilistic models.

by ONR under grants N00014-17-1-2889 and N00014-19-1-2318;
