and by the NSF under SaTC award 1801369, SHF awards 1812876 and 2007784, and CAREER
award 1845514.
Any opinions, findings, and conclusions or recommendations
expressed in this publication are those of the authors,
and do not necessarily reflect the views of the sponsoring
agencies.

Appendix A
Preliminaries on Measure Theory

Interested readers can refer to textbooks and notes in the
literature
(
Billingsley 2012
;
Williams 1991
)
for more details.

A
measurable space
is a pair
(S,\mathcal{S})
, where
S
is a nonempty set, and
\mathcal{S}
is a
\sigma
-algebra
on
S
, i.e., a family of subsets of
S
that contains
\emptyset
and is closed under complement and countable unions.
The smallest
\sigma
-algebra that contains a family
\mathcal{A}
of subsets of
S
is said to be
generated
by
\mathcal{A}
, denoted by
\sigma(\mathcal{A})
.
Every topological space
(S,\tau)
admits a
Borel
\sigma
-algebra
, given by
\sigma(\tau)
.
This gives canonical
\sigma
-algebras on
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathbb{N}
, etc.
A measurable space
(S,\mathcal{S})
is said to be a
standard Borel space
, if
\mathcal{S}
is a Borel

\sigma
-algebra generated by a complete metric space on
S
.
A measurable space
(S,\mathcal{S})
is a standard Borel space if and only if it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
or
a subset of
\mathbb{N}
.
A function
f:S\to T
, where
(S,\mathcal{S})
and
(T,\mathcal{T})
are measurable spaces, is said to be
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{T})
-measurable
, if
f^{-1}(B)\in\mathcal{S}
for each
B\in\mathcal{T}
.
If
T=\mathbb{R}
, we tacitly assume that the Borel
\sigma
-algebra is defined on
T
, and we simply call
f

measurable
, or a
random variable
.
Measurable functions form a vector space, and products, maxima, and limiting operations preserve measurability.

A
measure

\mu
on a measurable space
(S,\mathcal{S})
is a mapping from
\mathcal{S}
to
[0,\infty]
such that
(i)
\mu(\emptyset)=0
, and
(ii) for all pairwise-disjoint
\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
in
\mathcal{S}
, it holds that
\mu(\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}A_{i})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}\mu(A_{i})
.
The triple
(S,\mathcal{S},\mu)
is called a
measure space
.
A measure
\mu
is called a
probability
measure, if
\mu(S)=1
.
A measure
\mu
is called a
sub-probability
measure, if
\mu(S)\leq 1
.
A measure
\mu
is called
\sigma
-finite
, if
S
is the countable union of
measurable sets with finite measure.
We denote the collection of probability measures on
(S,\mathcal{S})
by
\mathbb{D}(S,\mathcal{S})
.
For each
x\in S
, the
Dirac measure

\delta(x)
is defined as
\lambda A.[x\in A]
.
For measures
\mu
and
\nu
, we write
\mu+\nu
for the measure
\lambda A.\mu(A)+\nu(A)
.
For measure
\mu
and scalar
c\geq 0
, we write
c\cdot\mu
for the measure
\lambda A.c\cdot\mu(A)
.

The
integral
of a measurable function
f
on
A\in\mathcal{S}
with respect to a measure
\mu
on
(S,\mathcal{S})
is defined following Lebesgue’s theory and is denoted by
\mu(f;A)
,
\int_{A}fd\mu
, or
\int_{A}f(x)\mu(dx)
.
If
A=S
, we tacitly omit
A
from the notations.
For each
A\in\mathcal{S}
, it holds that
\mu(f;A)=\mu(f\mathrm{I}_{A})
, where
\mathrm{I}_{A}
is the indicator function for
A
.

Let
f
be a nonnegative measurable function on
(S,\mathcal{S})
.
We can transform a measure
\mu
on
(S,\mathcal{S})
through
f
by integration:

f\mu\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\lambda A.\mu(f;A)
.
If
\nu
denotes the measure
f\mu
, we say that
\nu
has
density

f
relative to
\mu
,
and express this by
\frac{d\nu}{d\mu}=f
.
In this case, we have for
A\in\mathcal{S}
,
\mu(A)=0
implies that
\nu(A)=0
,
i.e.,
\nu
is
absolutely continuous
with respect to
\mu
.

A
kernel
from a measurable space
(S,\mathcal{S})
to another
(T,\mathcal{T})
is a mapping from
S\times\mathcal{T}
to
[0,\infty]
such that:
(i) for each
x\in S
, the function
\lambda B.\kappa(x,B)
is a measure on
(T,\mathcal{T})
, and
(ii) for each
B\in\mathcal{T}
, the function
\lambda x.\kappa(x,B)
is measurable.
We write
\kappa:(S,\mathcal{S})\rightsquigarrow(T,\mathcal{T})
to declare that
\kappa
is a kernel from
(S,\mathcal{S})
to
(T,\mathcal{T})
.
Intuitively, kernels describe measure transformers from one measurable space to another.
A kernel
\kappa
is called a
probability
kernel, if
\kappa(x,T)=1
for all
x\in S
.
We denote the collection of probability kernels from
(S,\mathcal{S})
to
(T,\mathcal{T})
by
\mathbb{K}((S,\mathcal{S}),(T,\mathcal{T}))
.
If the two measurable spaces coincide, we simply write
\mathbb{K}(S,\mathcal{S})
.
We can “push-forward” a measure
\mu
on
(S,\mathcal{S})
to a measure on
(T,\mathcal{T})
through a kernel
\kappa:(S,\mathcal{S})\rightsquigarrow(T,\mathcal{T})
by integration:
2
2

2

We use a
monad bind
notation
\mathbin{\gg\!=}
here.
Indeed, the category of measurable spaces admits a monad with sub-probability measures
(
Giry 1982
;
Panangaden 1999
)
.

\mu\mathbin{\gg\!=}\kappa\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\lambda B.\int_{S}\kappa(x,B)\mu(dx).

The
product
of two measurable spaces
(S,\mathcal{S})
and
(T,\mathcal{T})
is defined as
(S,\mathcal{S})\otimes(T,\mathcal{T})\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(S\times T,\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathcal{T})
, where
\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathcal{T}
is the smallest
\sigma
-algebra that makes coordinate maps measurable, i.e.,
\sigma(\{\pi_{1}^{-1}(A)\mid A\in\mathcal{S}\}\cup\{\pi_{2}^{-1}(B)\mid B\in\mathcal{T}\}))
, where
\pi_{i}
is the
i
-the coordinate map.
If
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are two measures on
(S,\mathcal{S})
and
(T,\mathcal{T})
, respectively,
then there exists a measure on
(S,\mathcal{S})\otimes(T,\mathcal{T})
, called the
product measure
and written

\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}
, such that
(\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2})(A\times B)=\mu_{1}(A)\mu_{2}(B)
.
When
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are
\sigma
-finite, the product measure is uniquely defined and also
\sigma
-finite.

The
coproduct
(i.e., disjoint union) of two measurable space
(S,\mathcal{S})
and
(T,\mathcal{T})
is defined as

(S,\mathcal{S})\amalg(T,\mathcal{T})\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(S\sqcup T,\mathcal{S}\amalg\mathcal{T})
, where

S\sqcup T\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\{i_{1}(x)\mid x\in S\}\cup\{i_{1}(y)\mid y\in T\}
,
i_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\lambda x.\langle 1,x\rangle
,

i_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\lambda y.\langle 2,y\rangle
, and

\mathcal{S}\amalg\mathcal{T}
is the smallest
\sigma
-algebra that makes injection maps measurable, i.e.,

\sigma(\{i_{1}^{-1}(A)\mid A\in\mathcal{S}\}\cup\{i_{2}^{-1}(B)\mid B\in\mathcal{T}\})=\{i_{1}^{-1}(A)\cup i_{2}^{-1}(B)\mid A\in\mathcal{S}\wedge B\in\mathcal{T}\}
.
If
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are two measures on
(S,\mathcal{S})
and
(T,\mathcal{T})
, respectively,
then we can define their
coproduct measure
, written
\mu_{1}\amalg\mu_{2}
,
as
(\mu_{1}\amalg\mu_{2})(i_{1}^{-1}(A)\cup i_{2}^{-1}(B))\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\mu_{1}(A)+\mu_{2}(B)
, for any
A\in\mathcal{S}
,

B\in\mathcal{T}
.
We can easily extend the binary coproducts to arbitary coproducts.
Particularly, the countable coproduct of
\sigma
-finite measures is still
\sigma
-finite.

Standard Borel spaces are closed under countable products and coproducts.
We will use this property in our construction of semantic domains in
appendix

C
.

presents a complete list of evaluation rules for expressions and commands.

presents a complete list of typing rules for expressions, commands, and programs.

Figure 13
.
Type rules for values, environments, and guidance traces.

Proposition B.1.

If
d:\mathsf{dist}(\tau)
and
v
is a value, then
v:\tau
if and only if
v\in d.\mathrm{support}
(i.e.,
d.\mathrm{density}(v)>0
).

Proof.

Appeal to mathematical properties of primitive distributions.
∎

Proposition B.2.

•

If
\Gamma\vdash e:\tau
,
V\vdash e\mathrel{\Downarrow}v
, and
V:\Gamma
, then
v:\tau
.

•

If
\Gamma\vdash e:\tau
,
V:\Gamma
, then there exists a value
v
such that
V\vdash e\mathrel{\Downarrow}v
.

Proof.

Appeal to type soundness and strong normalization of the simply-typed lambda calculus.
∎

Lemma B.3 (Substitution).

If
\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})
,
then for any
X_{a},X_{b},A_{o},B_{o}
, it holds that

\Gamma\vdash(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[A_{o}/X_{a}]A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[B_{o}/X_{b}]B)\vdash m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[A_{o}/X_{a}]A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[B_{o}/X_{b}]B^{\prime})
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})
.
We show several nontrivial cases; others are similar to one of these cases.

Case::

\small\frac{\Gamma\vdash e:\tau}{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash\mathsf{ret}(e)\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{TM:Ret}{({TM:Ret})}},}

\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[A_{o}/X_{a}]A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[B_{o}/X_{b}]B)\vdash\mathsf{ret}(e)\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[A_{o}/X_{a}]A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[B_{o}/X_{b}]B)
(
TM:Ret
)

Case::

\small\frac{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m_{1}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime\prime})\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,x:\tau_{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime\prime})\vdash m_{2}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})}{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash\mathsf{bnd}(m_{1};x.m_{2})\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{TM:Bnd}{({TM:Bnd})}},}

\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[A_{o}/X_{a}]A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[B_{o}/X_{b}]B)\vdash m_{1}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[A_{o}/X_{a}]A^{\prime\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[B_{o}/X_{b}]B^{\prime\prime})
(I.H.)

\Gamma,x:\tau_{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[A_{o}/X_{a}]A^{\prime\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[B_{o}/X_{b}]B^{\prime\prime})\vdash m_{2}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[A_{o}/X_{a}]A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[B_{o}/X_{b}]B^{\prime})
(I.H.)

\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[A_{o}/X_{a}]A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[B_{o}/X_{b}]B)\vdash\mathsf{bnd}(m_{1};x.m_{2})\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[A_{o}/X_{a}]A^{\prime\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[B_{o}/X_{b}]B^{\prime\prime})
(
TM:Bnd
)

Case::

\small\frac{\Sigma(f)=\tau_{1}\leadsto\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:T_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:T_{b})\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash e:\tau_{1}}{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:T_{a}[A^{\prime}]);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:T_{b}[B^{\prime}])\vdash\mathsf{call}(f;e)\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{TM:Call}{({TM:Call})}},}

,
B=T_{b}[B^{\prime}]
(assumption)

∎

Theorem B.4 (Well-typed programs evaluate to well-typed values).

If

\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})
,

V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v
, and

V:\Gamma
,
then

v:\tau
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v
, followed by inversion on
\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})
.
We show several nontrivial cases; others are similar to one of these cases.

Theorem B.5 (Well-typed programs produce well-typed traces).

If

\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})
,

V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v
,

V:\Gamma
,

\sigma_{a}^{\prime}:A^{\prime}
, and

\sigma_{b}^{\prime}:B^{\prime}
,
then

(\sigma_{a}\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{a}^{\prime}):A
and

(\sigma_{b}\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{b}^{\prime}):B
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v
, followed by inversion on
\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})
.
We show several nontrivial cases; others are similar to one of these cases.

Case::

\small\frac{V\vdash e\mathrel{\Downarrow}v}{V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:[]);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:[])\vdash\mathsf{ret}(e)\mathrel{\Downarrow^{1}}v}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{EM:Ret}{({EM:Ret})}},}\frac{\Gamma\vdash e:\tau}{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash\mathsf{ret}(e)\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{TM:Ret}{({TM:Ret})}},}

\sigma_{a}=[]
,
\sigma_{b}=[]
(assumption)

(\sigma_{a}\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{a}^{\prime})=\sigma_{a}^{\prime}
,
(\sigma_{b}\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{b}^{\prime})=\sigma_{b}^{\prime}

\sigma_{a}^{\prime}:A^{\prime}
,
\sigma_{b}^{\prime}:B^{\prime}
,
A=A^{\prime}
,
B=B^{\prime}
(assumption)

(\sigma_{a}\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{a}^{\prime}):A
,
(\sigma_{b}\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{b}^{\prime}):B

Case::

\small\frac{V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a,1});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b,1})\vdash m_{1}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{1}}}v_{1}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994ptV[x\mapsto v_{1}]\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a,2});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b,2})\vdash m_{2}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{2}}}v}{V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a,1}\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{a,2});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b,1}\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{b,2})\vdash\mathsf{bnd}(m_{1};x.m_{2})\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{1}\cdot w_{2}}}v}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{EM:Bnd}{({EM:Bnd})}},}\frac{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m_{1}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime\prime})\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,x:\tau_{1}\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime\prime})\vdash m_{2}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})}{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash\mathsf{bnd}(m_{1};x.m_{2})\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{TM:Bnd}{({TM:Bnd})}},}

v_{1}:\tau_{1}
(
theorem

B.4
)

V:\Gamma
(assumption)

V[x\mapsto v_{1}]:(\Gamma,x:\tau_{1})
(
TC:Extend

\sigma_{a}^{\prime}:A^{\prime}
,

(assumption)

Corollary 0 (
theorem

4.4
).

If
\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash_{\Sigma}m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\boldsymbol{1})

∎

Theorem B.6 (Normalization, part I).

If

\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})
,

V:\Gamma
,

\sigma_{a}:A
,
and
\sigma_{b}:B
,
then
there exist
w,v,\sigma_{a}^{\prime\prime},\sigma_{a}^{\prime},\sigma_{b}^{\prime\prime},\sigma_{b}^{\prime}
such that

V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a}^{\prime\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b}^{\prime\prime})\vdash m\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v
,

\sigma_{a}=\sigma_{a}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{a}^{\prime}
,

\sigma_{b}=\sigma_{b}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\mathbin{{+}\!\!{+}}}\sigma_{b}^{\prime}
,

\sigma_{a}^{\prime}:A^{\prime}
,
and
\sigma_{b}^{\prime}:B^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By nested induction on the derivation of
\sigma_{a}:A
,
\sigma_{b}:B
, and
\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})
.
We show several nontrivial cases; others are similar to one of these cases.

Theorem B.7 (Normalization, part II).

If

\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})
,

V:\Gamma
,

A
is
\mathbin{\binampersand}
-free,

B
is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free,

\sigma_{a}:A
,
and
\sigma_{b}:B
,
then

A^{\prime}
is
\mathbin{\binampersand}
-free,

B^{\prime}
is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free,
and there exist
w,v,\sigma_{a}^{\prime\prime},\sigma_{a}^{\prime},\sigma_{b}^{\prime\prime},\sigma_{b}^{\prime}

,

,

.

Corollary 0 (
theorem

4.6
).

If

\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\boldsymbol{1})
,

is
\mathbin{\binampersand}
-free,

is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free,

\sigma_{a}:A
,
and
\sigma_{b}:B

there exist

such that

\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v
,

v:\tau
,
and
w>0
.

Proof.

theorems

B.7
and
B.4
.
∎

To justify that our operational semantics correctly keeps track of
possible
traces for running a program,
we introduce a reduction relation
V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\mathrel{\vdash_{\mathsf{red}}}m\mathrel{\Downarrow}v
that ignores all the information about probabilities.

presents reduction rules for commands.

Reduction rules for commands.

Theorem B.8.

Suppose that

\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})
and

V:\Gamma
.
Then for
any
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b},v
,
we have

V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\mathrel{\vdash_{\mathsf{red}}}m\mathrel{\Downarrow}v

if and only if

V\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v
for some
w>0
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of the evaluation judgment,
followed by inversion on
\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A^{\prime});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B^{\prime})
.

We show several nontrivial cases; others are similar to one of these cases.

\small\frac{\Gamma\vdash e:\tau}{\Gamma\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash\mathsf{ret}(e)\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{3.68121pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.19376pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={\hypertarget{TM:Ret}{({TM:Ret})}},}

Corollary B.9.

Suppose that

\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\boldsymbol{1})
,
Then for any
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b},v
,
we have

\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\mathrel{\vdash_{\mathsf{red}}}m\mathrel{\Downarrow}v

if and only if

\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v
for some
w>0
.

Proof.

Appeal to
theorem

B.8
.
∎

Appendix C
Full Development of Sound Bayesian Inference

Semantic domains

For each scalar type
\tau
, we equip it with a standard Borel space
\llbracket\tau\rrbracket
on inhabitants of
\tau
:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbb{1}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(\{\mathsf{triv}\},\wp(\{\mathsf{triv}\}))

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbb{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(\{\mathsf{true},\mathsf{false}\},\wp(\{\mathsf{true},\mathsf{false}\}))

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}((0,1),\mathcal{B}(0,1))

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbb{R}_{+}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}((0,\infty),\mathcal{B}(0,\infty))

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbb{R}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(({-\infty},\infty),\mathcal{B}({-\infty},\infty))

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbb{N}_{n}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(\{0,1,\cdots,n-1\},\wp(\{0,1,\cdots,n-1\}))

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbb{N}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(\{0,1,\cdots\},\wp(\{0,1,\cdots\}))

For each guide type
A
, we construct a standard Borel space
\llbracket A\rrbracket
on guidance traces of type
A
.
We first present a parameterized construction
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})}
, where
(C,\mathcal{C})
is a standard Borel
space, for guide types
excluding
type-level applications:

\displaystyle\llbracket\boldsymbol{1}\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(C,\mathcal{C})\

\displaystyle\llbracket\tau\wedge A\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\llbracket\tau\rrbracket)\otimes\llbracket A\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})}

\displaystyle\llbracket\tau\supset A\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{C}}(\llbracket\tau\rrbracket)\otimes\llbracket A\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})}

\displaystyle\llbracket A\mathbin{\varoplus}B\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\mathsf{true})\otimes\llbracket A\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})})\amalg(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\mathsf{false})\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})})

\displaystyle\llbracket A\mathbin{\binampersand}B\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathsf{true})\otimes\llbracket A\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})})\amalg(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathsf{false})\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})})

We use product measurable spaces to construct trace spaces by treating a trace as a pair of its head and tail,
and coproduct measurable spaces to join trace spaces from different branches.
Then, for every type definition
\mathsf{typedef}(T.X.A)
, we define a function
f_{T}
that maps a standard Borel space to another one:

f_{T}\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\lambda(C,\mathcal{C}).\textbf{{fold}}\otimes\llbracket A\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})}.

We can now construct a map
F_{T}
that computes fixed points:

F_{T}\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\lambda(C,\mathcal{C}).\coprod_{n=0}^{\infty}f_{T}^{n}(C,\mathcal{C}).

Because standard Borel spaces are closed under countable coproducts, we know that
F_{T}
is
well-defined.
We can then add the construction for type-level applications:

\displaystyle\llbracket T[B]\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}F_{T}(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{(C,\mathcal{C})}).

For closed programs, we usually set the continuation space
(C,\mathcal{C})
to
(\{[]\},\wp(\{[]\}))
.
Thus, we obtain the following definitions:

\displaystyle\llbracket\boldsymbol{1}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(\{[]\},\wp(\{[]\}))

\displaystyle\llbracket\tau\wedge A\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\llbracket\tau\rrbracket)\otimes\llbracket A\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\tau\supset A\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{C}}(\llbracket\tau\rrbracket)\otimes\llbracket A\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket A\mathbin{\varoplus}B\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\mathsf{true})\otimes\llbracket A\rrbracket)\amalg(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\mathsf{false})\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket)

\displaystyle\llbracket A\mathbin{\binampersand}B\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathsf{true})\otimes\llbracket A\rrbracket)\amalg(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathsf{false})\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket)

\displaystyle\llbracket T[B]\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}F_{T}(\llbracket B\rrbracket)

Stock measures

For each scalar type
\tau
, we equip it with a stock measure
\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket}
on its semantic domain

\llbracket\tau\rrbracket
.
For nullary products
\mathbb{1}
, Booleans
\mathbb{2}
, integer rings
\mathbb{N}_{n}
, and natural numbers
\mathbb{N}
,
we define
\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket}
to be the counting measure, i.e.,
\lambda S.|S|
.
For unit interval
\mathbb{R}_{(0,1)}
, positive real line
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and real line
\mathbb{R}
,
we define
\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket}
to be the Lebesgue measure
\mathbf{Leb}
, i.e., the
unique measure that satisfies
\mathbf{Leb}([a,b])=b-a
for any interval
[a,b]
.
All these measures are
\sigma
-finite.

For each guide type
A
, we construct a stock measure
\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}
on its semantic domain

\llbracket A\rrbracket
.
Similar to the construction of semantic domains, we first present a parameterized construction

\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket;{\mu_{C}}}
, where
\mu_{C}
is a measure on a standard Borel space
(C,\mathcal{C})
,
for guide types
excluding
type-level applications:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\llbracket 1\rrbracket;{\mu_{C}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\mu_{C}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\wedge A\rrbracket;{\mu_{C}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket})\otimes\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket;{\mu_{C}}}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\supset A\rrbracket;{\mu_{C}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{C}}(\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket})\otimes\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket;{\mu_{C}}}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\llbracket A\mathbin{\varoplus}B\rrbracket;\mu_{C}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\mathsf{true})\otimes\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket;\mu_{C}})\amalg(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\mathsf{false})\otimes\lambda_{\llbracket B\rrbracket;\mu_{C}})

\displaystyle\lambda_{\llbracket A\mathbin{\binampersand}B\rrbracket;\mu_{C}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathsf{true})\otimes\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket;\mu_{C}})\amalg(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathsf{false})\otimes\lambda_{\llbracket B\rrbracket;\mu_{C}})

We use product measures to construct sequencing trace spaces, and coproduct measures to join measures for
trace spaces from different branches.
Then, for every type definition
\mathsf{typedef}(T.X.A)
, we define a function
\mathscr{f}_{T}
that maps a measure to another one:

\mathscr{f}_{T}\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\lambda(\mu_{C}).\textbf{{fold}}\otimes\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket;\mu_{C}}.

We can now construct a map
\mathscr{F}_{T}
that computes fixed points:

\mathscr{F}_{T}\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\lambda(\mu_{C}).\coprod_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathscr{f}_{T}^{n}(\mu_{C}).

Because arbitrary coproduct of measures is well-defined, we know that
\mathscr{F}_{T}
is also well-defined.
We can then add the construction for type-level applications:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\llbracket T[B]\rrbracket;{\mu_{C}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\mathscr{F}_{T}(\lambda_{\llbracket B\rrbracket;{\mu_{C}}}).

Finally, we drop the
\mu_{C}
parameter to obtain the following constructions:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\llbracket\boldsymbol{1}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\text{counting measure on $\llbracket\boldsymbol{1}\rrbracket$}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\wedge A\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket})\otimes\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\supset A\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\textbf{{val}}^{\mathsf{C}}(\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket})\otimes\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\llbracket A\mathbin{\varoplus}B\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\mathsf{true})\otimes\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket})\amalg(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{P}}(\mathsf{false})\otimes\lambda_{\llbracket B\rrbracket})

\displaystyle\lambda_{\llbracket A\mathbin{\binampersand}B\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathsf{true})\otimes\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket})\amalg(\textbf{{dir}}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathsf{false})\otimes\lambda_{\llbracket B\rrbracket})

\displaystyle\lambda_{\llbracket T[B]\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\mathscr{F}_{T}(\lambda_{\llbracket B\rrbracket})

In addition,
\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}
is a
\sigma
-finite measure for any guide type
A
, because
(i) the stock measure
\lambda_{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket}
for any scalar type
\tau
is
\sigma
-finite,
(ii) binary product of
\sigma
-finite measures is still
\sigma
-finite, and
(ii) countable coproduct of
\sigma
-finite measures still
\sigma
-finite.

Denotation of commands

For a well-typed closed command
m
, i.e.,

\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{b}}\!:B)\vdash_{\Sigma}m\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\boldsymbol{1})
,
we define the
density function
of
m
as

\mathbf{P}_{m}(\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b})\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\begin{cases}w&if $\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{a}}\!:\sigma_{a});(\textnormal{{b}}\!:\sigma_{b})\vdash m\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v$\\
0&otherwise\end{cases}.

Proposition C.1.

\mathbf{P}_{m}
is measurable.

Proof.

We follow the proof strategy of
Borgström et al. 2016
, where they proved measurability of density functions
in an untyped probabilistic lambda calculus.
∎

Then, we construct a measure denotation
\llbracket m\rrbracket
for
m
, by integrating
\mathbf{P}_{m}
with
respect to the stock measure on the product space
\llbracket A\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket
, i.e.,

\llbracket m\rrbracket(S_{a,b})\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\int_{S_{a,b}}\mathbf{P}_{m}(\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket}(d(\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b})),

where
S_{a,b}
is a measurable set in
\llbracket A\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket
.

Bayesian inference

Let us fix a well-typed model program
m_{\mathsf{m}}
that consumes latent random variables on
a channel
latent
and provides observations on a channel
obs
, i.e.,

\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:B)\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{m}}\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}).

Usually, the program
m_{\mathsf{m}}
does
not
receive any branch selections, i.e.,

A
is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free and
B
is
\mathbin{\binampersand}
-free.
Given a concrete observation
\sigma_{o}:B
,
Bayesian inference is the problem of approximating the
posterior

\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}
,
a measure
conditioned
with respect to
\sigma_{o}
, defined by

\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}(S_{\ell})\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\frac{\int_{S_{\ell}}\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})}{\int\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})},

where
S_{\ell}
is a measurable set in
\llbracket A\rrbracket
, i.e., a set of guidance traces of type
A
.

Guide programs

In our system, we implement a guide program
m_{\mathsf{g}}
as
a coroutine that works with the model program
m_{\mathsf{m}}
and provides the

latent
channel with guide type
A
that
m_{\mathsf{m}}
consumes, i.e.,

\displaystyle\cdot\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A)\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{g}}\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}),

\displaystyle\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:B)\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{m}}\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}).

The coroutine-based paradigm folds the model and guide programs into a single entity;
thus, during the inference, both the model and guide coroutines execute.
However, to distinguish the two measures defined by the model and guide, respectively,
we define a denotation for the guide
m_{\mathsf{g}}
, accompanied by the model
m_{\mathsf{m}}
and conditioned
on a concrete observation
\sigma_{o}:B
, as a measure defined by

\llbracket m_{\mathsf{g}}\rrbracket^{m_{\mathsf{m}}}_{\sigma_{o}}(S_{\ell})\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\int_{S_{\ell}}[\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\neq 0]\cdot\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{g}}}(\sigma_{\ell})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell}),

where
S_{\ell}
is a measurable set in
\llbracket A\rrbracket
, i.e., a set of guidance traces of type
A
.

To justify the inclusion of
[\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\neq 0]
in the denotation of the guide program, we consider
possible
traces for a model-guide system.
A combination of traces
(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})
is said to be

possible
for the model program
m_{\mathsf{m}}
and the guide program
m_{\mathsf{g}}
, if

\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\mathrel{\vdash_{\mathsf{red}}}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}v_{\mathsf{m}}
and
\emptyset\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell})\mathrel{\vdash_{\mathsf{red}}}m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}v_{\mathsf{g}}
for some values
v_{\mathsf{m}}
and
v_{\mathsf{g}}
.

Lemma 0 (
lemma

5.1
).

Suppose that
A
is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free,
B
is
\mathbin{\binampersand}
-free, and

\displaystyle\cdot\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{g}}\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}),

\displaystyle\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:B)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{m}}\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}).

Then a combination of traces
(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})
is possible for the model
m_{\mathsf{m}}
and the guide
m_{\mathsf{g}}

if and only if

\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\neq 0
.

Proof.

•

The “if” direction:

\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\neq 0
(assumption)

\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\vdash m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v
for some
v,w
such that
w>0
(definition)

\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\mathrel{\vdash_{\mathsf{red}}}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}v
(
corollary

B.9
)

A
is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free (assumption)

\emptyset\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell})\vdash m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w^{\prime}}}v^{\prime}
for some
v^{\prime},w^{\prime}
such that
w^{\prime}>0
(
theorem

4.6
)

\emptyset\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell})\mathrel{\vdash_{\mathsf{red}}}m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}v^{\prime}
(
corollary

B.9
)

•

The “only if” direction:

\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\mathrel{\vdash_{\mathsf{red}}}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}v
for some
v
(assumption)

\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\vdash m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w}}v
for some
w>0
(
corollary

B.9
)

\sigma_{\ell}:A
,
\sigma_{o}:B
(
theorem

4.4
)

\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})=w>0
(definition)

∎

Absolute continuity

Recall that a measure
\mu
is said to be
absolutely continuous
with respect to a measure
\nu
,
if
\mu
and
\nu
are defined on the same measurable space, and

\nu(S)\neq 0
for every measurable set
S
for which
\mu(S)\neq 0
.

We prove that for a model-guide pair, guide types serve as certificates for
absolute continuity.

Theorem 0 (
theorem

5.2
).

Suppose that

\displaystyle\cdot\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{g}}\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}),

\displaystyle\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:B)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{m}}\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}),

A
is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free,
B
is
\mathbin{\binampersand}
-free,
and
\sigma_{o}:B
such that
\int\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})>0
.
Then
the measure
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
the measure
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{g}}\rrbracket^{m_{\mathsf{m}}}_{\sigma_{o}}
, and vice versa.

Proof.

We first claim that for any
\sigma_{\ell}\in\llbracket A\rrbracket
, it holds that
\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\neq 0
if and only if
[\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\neq 0]\cdot\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{g}}}(\sigma_{\ell})\neq 0
.

(
\implies
):

Assume
\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\neq 0
.
Thus, there exists
v_{1}
such that
\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\vdash m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{1}}}v_{1}
, where
w_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})
.
Because
A
is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free, we can apply
theorem

4.6
to program
m_{\mathsf{g}}
.
Thus, there exist
w_{2},v_{2}
such that
\emptyset\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell})\vdash m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{2}}}v_{2}
and
w_{2}>0
.
In other words, we have
\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{g}}}(\sigma_{\ell})>0
.
Then, we conclude that
[\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\neq 0]\cdot\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{g}}}(\sigma_{\ell})\neq 0
.

(
\impliedby
):

Assume
[\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\neq 0]\cdot\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{g}}}(\sigma_{\ell})\neq 0
.
Thus, we have both
\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\neq 0
and
\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{g}}}(\sigma_{\ell})\neq 0
.
Then, we conclude that
\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\neq 0
directly.

Fix a measurable set
S_{\ell}
in
\llbracket A\rrbracket
.
It suffices to show that
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}(S_{\ell})=0
if and only if
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{g}}\rrbracket^{m_{\mathsf{m}}}_{\sigma_{o}}(S_{\ell})=0
.
We conclude by the following reasoning:

\displaystyle\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}(S_{\ell})=0

\displaystyle\iff\int_{S_{\ell}}\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})=0

\displaystyle\iff\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}({\cdot},\sigma_{o})\cdot\mathrm{I}_{S_{\ell}})=0

\displaystyle\iff\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(\{\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}({\cdot},\sigma_{o})\cdot\mathrm{I}_{S_{\ell}}>0\})=0

\displaystyle\iff\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(\{\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}({\cdot},\sigma_{o})>0\wedge\mathrm{I}_{S_{\ell}}>0\})=0

\displaystyle\iff\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(\{([\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}({\cdot},\sigma_{o})\neq 0]\cdot\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{g}}}({\cdot}))>0\wedge\mathrm{I}_{S_{\ell}}>0\})=0

\displaystyle\iff\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(\{[\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}({\cdot},\sigma_{o})\neq 0]\cdot\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{g}}}({\cdot})\cdot\mathrm{I}_{S_{\ell}}>0\})=0

\displaystyle\iff\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}([\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}({\cdot},\sigma_{o})\neq 0]\cdot\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{g}}}({\cdot})\cdot\mathrm{I}_{S_{\ell}})=0

\displaystyle\iff\int_{S_{\ell}}[\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\neq 0]\cdot\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{g}}}(\sigma_{\ell})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})=0

\displaystyle\iff\llbracket m_{\mathsf{g}}\rrbracket^{m_{\mathsf{m}}}_{\sigma_{o}}(S_{\ell})=0.

∎

Importance sampling (IS)

Recall the operational rule below for a single step in the IS algorithm:
given a model program
m_{\mathsf{m}}
, a guide program
m_{\mathsf{g}}
, and a concrete observation
\sigma_{o}
,
IS performs joint execution of the two programs to draw a sample
\sigma_{\ell}
with density
w_{\mathsf{g}}

and compute
\frac{w_{\mathsf{m}}}{w_{\mathsf{g}}}
as the importance of
\sigma_{\ell}
.

\frac{\emptyset\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell})\vdash m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{\mathsf{g}}}}\_\quad\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\vdash m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{\mathsf{m}}}}\_}{m_{\mathsf{g}};m_{\mathsf{m}};\sigma_{o}\vdash_{\textsc{is}}^{w_{\mathsf{g}}}\langle\sigma_{\ell},\nicefrac{{w_{\mathsf{m}}}}{{w_{\mathsf{g}}}}\rangle}

Define a density function
\textsc{is}(\sigma_{\ell})\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}w_{\mathsf{g}}\cdot\frac{w_{\mathsf{m}}}{w_{\mathsf{g}}}=w_{\mathsf{m}}
on
the space
\llbracket A\rrbracket
of guidance traces for the computation of IS.
Then, the measure defined by IS can be defined as
\mu_{\textsc{is}}(S_{\ell})\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\int_{S_{\ell}}\textsc{is}(\sigma_{\ell})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})
.

Lemma C.2.

Suppose that

\displaystyle\cdot\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{g}}\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}),

\displaystyle\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:B)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{m}}\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}),

A
is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free,
B
is
\mathbin{\binampersand}
-free,
and
\sigma_{o}:B
such that
\int\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})>0
.
Then
\mu_{\textnormal{{is}}}\propto\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}
.

Proof.

By
theorem

5.2
, we know that the posterior
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to

\llbracket m_{\mathsf{g}}\rrbracket^{m_{\mathsf{m}}}_{\sigma_{o}}
.
Thus,
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}
is also absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu_{\textsc{is}}
.
In other words, the density function
\textsc{is}({\cdot})
is positive at all possible latent variables

\sigma_{\ell}
in the posterior.
Then, for any
S_{\ell}
such that
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}(S_{\ell})\neq 0
,
we have

\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}(S_{\ell})=\frac{\int_{S_{\ell}}\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})}{\int\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})}=\frac{\int_{S_{\ell}}\textsc{is}(\sigma_{\ell})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})}{\int\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})}=\frac{\mu_{\textsc{is}}(S_{\ell})}{\int\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})},

and we conclude by the fact the the denominator is a constant.
∎

Variational inference (VI)

Recall that we parameterize the guide program
m_{\mathsf{g},\theta}
by a vector
\theta\in\Theta
of parameters,
and use KL divergence as the distance metric, which is defined by

\mathrm{KL}(\mu\parallel\nu)\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\int p_{\mu}(\sigma_{\ell})(\log p_{\mu}(\sigma_{\ell})-\log p_{\nu}(\sigma_{\ell}))\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell}),

where
\mu
and
\nu
are measures on a space
\llbracket A\rrbracket
of guidance traces of type
A
with
densities
p_{\mu}
and
p_{\nu}
, respectively,
and
\mu
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\nu
.
The rule below formulates the computation of KL divergence for a specific
\theta
,
via joint execution of the two programs.

\frac{\emptyset\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell})\vdash m_{\mathsf{g},\theta}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{\mathsf{g}}}}\_\quad\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\vdash m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{\mathsf{m}}}}\_}{m_{\mathsf{g},\theta};m_{\mathsf{m}};\sigma_{o}\vdash_{\textsc{vi}}^{w_{\mathsf{g}}}\langle\sigma_{\ell},\log w_{\mathsf{m}}-\log w_{\mathsf{g}}\rangle}

The rule can be seen as defining a map
\sigma_{\ell}\mapsto w_{\mathsf{g}}\cdot(\log w_{\mathsf{m}}-\log w_{\mathsf{g}})
,
which is the integrand of the divergence
\mathrm{KL}(\llbracket m_{\mathsf{g},\theta}\rrbracket^{m_{\mathsf{m}}}_{\sigma_{o}}\parallel\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}})
.

Lemma C.3.

Suppose that

\displaystyle\cdot\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{g},\theta}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{g}}\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}),

\displaystyle\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:B)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{m}}\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}),

A
is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free,
B
is
\mathbin{\binampersand}
-free,
and
\sigma_{o}:B
such that
\int\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})>0
.
Then,
\mathrm{KL}(\llbracket m_{\mathsf{g},\theta}\rrbracket^{m_{\mathsf{m}}}_{\sigma_{o}}\parallel\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}})

is well-defined.

Proof.

By
theorem

5.2
, we know that
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{g},\theta}\rrbracket^{m_{\mathsf{m}}}_{\sigma_{o}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}
.
Thus, the KL divergence used in VI is well-defined.
∎

Markov-Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC)

We focus on
Metropolis-Hastings
(MH), which constructs the transition
kernel from a
proposal
subroutine, and a probabilistic decision subroutine that either
accepts the proposed random sample, or rejects it and keeps the old one.
To implement proposal subroutines in our system, we extend the core calculus such that guidance
traces can be used as first-class data.
Then we implement the proposal subroutine as a procedure
g
whose argument is a guidance trace
on the channel for latent random variables.
The operational rule below formulates a single step in the MH algorithm;
given a proposal procedure
g
, a model program
m_{\mathsf{m}}
, a concrete observation
\sigma_{o}
,
and the current latent trace
\sigma_{\ell}
, MH first performs joint execution of
\mathsf{call}(g;\sigma_{\ell})

and
m_{\mathsf{m}}
to generate a new latent trace
\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime}
with density
w_{\mathsf{fwd}}
,
and then uses the new
\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime}
and the old
\sigma_{\ell}
to calculate a
backward
density
w_{\mathsf{bwd}}
.
MH then uses these densities to compute an
acceptance ratio

\alpha
, and then accepts
the new sample
\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime}
with probability
\alpha
.

\frac{\emptyset\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime})\vdash\mathsf{call}(g;\sigma_{\ell})\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{\mathsf{fwd}}}}\_\quad\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\vdash m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w^{\prime}_{\mathsf{m}}}}\_\quad\emptyset\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell})\vdash\mathsf{call}(g;\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime})\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{\mathsf{bwd}}}}\_\quad\emptyset\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\sigma_{\ell});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\sigma_{o})\vdash m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathrel{\Downarrow^{w_{\mathsf{m}}}}\_\quad\alpha=\min(1,\frac{w^{\prime}_{\mathsf{m}}\cdot w_{\mathsf{bwd}}}{w_{\mathsf{m}}\cdot w_{\mathsf{fwd}}})}{g;m_{\mathsf{m}};\sigma_{o}\vdash_{\textsc{mh}}\sigma_{\ell}\xRightarrow{w_{\mathsf{fwd}}\cdot\alpha}\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime}}

The MH algorithm specifies a transition density
f_{1}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime})\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}w_{\mathsf{fwd}}\cdot\alpha
and
an unchanged density
f_{2}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{\ell}^{\prime})\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}w_{\mathsf{fwd}}\cdot(1-\alpha)
.
Then we can use the two density functions to construct the MH kernel

\kappa_{\textsc{mh}}(\sigma,S)\mathrel{\overset{\underset{\textnormal{def}}{}}{=}}\int_{S}(f_{1}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})+f_{2}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})\cdot[\sigma\in S])\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma^{\prime})
.

Lemma C.4.

Suppose that

\displaystyle\textnormal{{old}}:|A|\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{g}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{g}}\mid\varnothing;(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}),

\displaystyle\cdot\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:A);(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:B)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\Sigma}m_{\mathsf{m}}\mathbin{\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{4.09024pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr$\sim$\cr\vphantom{$\div$}\raisebox{-0.21529pt}{\scalebox{1.2}{.}}\cr}}}}\tau_{\mathsf{m}}\mid(\textnormal{{latent}}\!:\boldsymbol{1});(\textnormal{{obs}}\!:\boldsymbol{1}),

m_{\mathsf{g}}
is the procedure body of the proposal program
g
, which has a single parameter
old
with
type
|A|
that describes first-class guidance traces,

A
is
\mathbin{\varoplus}
-free,
B
is
\mathbin{\binampersand}
-free,
and
\sigma_{o}:B
such that
\int\mathbf{P}_{m_{\mathsf{m}}}(\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{o})\lambda_{\llbracket A\rrbracket}(d\sigma_{\ell})>0
.
Then the posterior
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}
is stationary for the kernel
\kappa_{\textnormal{{mh}}}
.

Proof.

We can extend
theorem

5.2
by allowing the environments to contain values and
then using
theorem

B.7
instead of
theorem

4.6
in
the proof.
Then, we derive that for any latent variables
\sigma
,
the posterior
\llbracket m_{\mathsf{m}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{o}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
the measure
\kappa_{\textsc{mh}}(\sigma,{\cdot})
;
that is, the guide program is able to sample any latent variables in the posterior,
no matter what the current sample is.
We then conclude by applying the Metropolis-Hastings-Green theorem
(
Green 1995
)
.
∎
